He had built that on the Sunday, the same way he built the sentence for the house on the hill, and he had taken two things out of it and one of them had been the word *opportunity*.

Mr Kwon did not say anything for about eight seconds.

"Say the first part again."

"Seven years. The same paper as the anchor cargo at Yeongjong, drawn by the same man."

"I know what that paper looks like. A cousin of my wife's carries under it." He had not moved. "That paper is why he has a terminal and everybody else has a berth."

"Why five."

"Because I am not going to bring him the sixth one."

Mr Kwon put both forearms on the desk.

"That is a very careful sentence."

"It is an exact one. I am telling you what I am going to do and not what is going to happen to her, because I do not know the second thing and I would be guessing."

"Then why."

"Ask her."

The room did the thing rooms do.

"That is all you are going to say."

"That is all I am going to say about it, and I would like you to notice that I have not said one word against her and I am not going to." Georgij kept his hands where they could be seen. "Ask her. She will tell you the truth, because she does not lie, and you have known that about her for twenty years and it is the only reason this is going to work."

Mr Kwon sat back.

"You are a very unpleasant man."

"Today, yes."

"I do not know what you are," he said. "You came in a car that costs more than my house. You knew about my apron before you knew about my trucks. You will not say what you want and you will not say who you are, and I have been in a room with two men in my life who did that and both of them were selling something."

"I have no title," said Georgij. "No company, no salary, no account, and nothing at all that I can sign. There is no piece of paper anywhere in this country with my name on it and a position underneath it."

"That is not an answer."

"It is the whole of the answer. There is nothing about me to check." He did not soften it. "Chairman Woo will confirm every word I have said to you about the seven years and about the five. He will not confirm anything about me, because there is nothing to confirm, and if he tries he will say something vague and you will hear him do it."

Mr Kwon looked at him for a long moment.

Then he picked up the telephone on the desk, which is a real telephone with a cord, and he did not go into another room to do it.

It took nine minutes.

Georgij sat where he was and looked at the ring binders and listened to one half of it, which is the half a man can afford to let somebody hear.

He heard Woo say the number of years, twice, because Mr Kwon made him say it twice.

He heard the phrase *a young man who works for somebody I do business with*.

He heard *I am not going to tell you any more than that*.

And after something he could not hear at all, he heard *I would not believe me either*.

He heard Mr Kwon laugh at that, once, and it was not a friendly laugh but it was a real one.

"He says the rate is real," said Mr Kwon, putting it down.

"It is real."

"He also says that if I ask him about you again he will hang up on me, and that I should ask you, and that you will tell me the truth and it will not help."

"Both of those are correct."

Mr Kwon got up and went to the window, which looks at nine trucks and room for eleven.

"My partner wants out," he said. "He has wanted out since his wife died and he cannot go, because there is nobody on this coast to sell his half to and I cannot buy it with what we make on this lane in a year."

"I know."

"Of course you know. Somebody asked my drivers." He did not turn round. "With seven years of that paper he can go. Somebody in Busan buys the half at a price that is a real price. He goes and lives near his daughter. And I stop watching a man of sixty-three come to work every day for a reason he stopped having."

Georgij did not say anything to that at all.

There was nothing available that was both true and useful, and everything available that was merely useful would have been a sale.

What he did instead was write four words in the notebook in the car, and they are the second half of a thing he said to Annie in the small room on the third of April.

Seven years of that paper does not only feed a firm. It makes a firm worth something. It makes a half of a firm saleable, and it does that to five firms inside a fortnight.

And Jang found the way into every one of them, in four pages, without being asked for it.

The man with nine trucks has a partner of sixty-three who has wanted out since his wife died and cannot go, because there is nobody on that coast who buys anything.

The man with three trucks has seven months left on a loan against his own yard and a son whose term begins in September.

The two brothers have not spoken since 2019 and would each of them sooner sell to a stranger than agree with the other about anything, and the invoice clerk has understood that for four years.

The fourth has been quietly moving work off that lane since February, which is a man who has already decided to go and is waiting to be able to afford it.

And Cho, who is forty, took a firm from his father in the autumn, and has spent six months finding out what his father actually was, and has not wanted it since about January.

Five men and five separate afternoons, and on every one of those afternoons there is going to be exactly one buyer on that coast with money in her hand.

She will pay a real price each time, because a bad price is a thing a man tells people about for twenty years. Every one of the five will believe it was the best fortnight of his working life, and every one of them will be right.

*Not customers. Not an arrangement.* All five, and not four, and not the two easy ones.

That is what he promised her on the third of April. It was not a figure of speech, and Woo's seven years are what make it possible, and Woo will never know that part. There is nothing in it that costs him anything and no reason to tell a man a thing he cannot use.

"You have not offered me anything," said Mr Kwon after a while.

"No."

"Why not."

"Because there is nothing I could offer you that you will not do for your own reasons inside a week. And because a man who takes something from me on a Tuesday spends the rest of his life wondering what it cost." Georgij stood up. "You have got a man coming at eleven who is not going to buy anything."

At the door Mr Kwon said the thing that Georgij had been waiting for since the apron.

"The constitution," he said. "Ours. There is a clause in it about who may speak for the members in a dealing with a carrier."

"There is."

"I have never read it. Twenty years and I have never read it, because there has never been a carrier."

"There is one now," said Georgij.

He got as far as the car.

"One thing," said Mr Kwon from the doorway. "You said ask her. You did not say what to ask her about."

"No."

"Because if you had, it would be your question."

"Yes."

Mr Kwon stood in his own doorway with his hands in his pockets and looked at him for a moment.

"I said you were an unpleasant man and you said today. Was that true."

There it was, and Mr Kwon had built it well and had probably not meant to.

"No," said Georgij. "I have been this for about three weeks and I do not know yet whether it goes away."

He got in the car and Mr Ku pulled out onto a road with a fuel depot on one side of it, and neither of them said anything for a long time.

Then Mr Ku said something on the expressway, about forty minutes out, which he has not done since October.

"That was a good yard."

"It was."

"My wife's brother drove for a firm with a yard like that. Twelve years, in Gimhae." He was watching the road and not the mirror. "Then it was a different firm and the yard was the same and he was not there any more."

Georgij looked at the back of his head for a moment.

"Is that a question."

"No," said Mr Ku.

He did not say anything else for the rest of it, and he was not sulking, and at the house he got out and opened the door the way he does.

Georgij carried that up the drive with him and has not put it down since.

He did not go and tell her about the day.

That is the arrangement now, and it is eleven days old, and this is the first week since October in which he has done a week's work and said nothing at all about it to anybody.

It turns out to be quieter than he had expected, and not in the way he had expected.

* * *

The four remaining firms did not need a visit and did not get one.

Woo's lawyer wrote to all five of them on the Thursday, on the same day, in the same words, and asked each of them the same three questions. What tonnage can you commit. What is your insurance position. **And: who is authorised to enter into an agreement on your behalf, and has anybody done so in the last six months.**

That is the third question every lawyer asks and nobody thinks about, and it went out to five men who had never in their lives been asked it.

By the Friday afternoon three of them had gone and read the constitution.

By the Saturday two of them had telephoned Nam Byung-hee.

Georgij does not know what she said on either call and did not try to find out.

He does not need it. She told them the truth, because that is what she does, and she did not soften it, because she has never once in twenty years softened anything.

It will have taken her under a minute each time.

The man outside the yard reported on the Sunday morning and had nothing, which is what Georgij had expected and had asked for anyway.

Nobody came. Nobody has come to that yard in a week who was not delivering something or driving for her. There is no car on that road that does not belong on it.

One line at the bottom of it, put there because the man is good at his job and not because anybody wanted it.

On the Saturday evening she did not go home. The light in the office over the weighbridge was on until half past ten and the yard was empty. At some point in the middle of it she came out and stood on the apron. She looked at four trucks for a while, and then she went back in.

By the Monday all five had written back to the lawyer, and four of the five had answered the third question in more words than it needed.

The fifth was Mr Kwon. He answered the third question in nine words. Then he telephoned the chair of his association and asked for a meeting of the members. In writing. Under a clause he had read for the first time on the Friday morning, at his own desk, with the door shut.

---

# Book Two · Chapter 15: One word, and it was true
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 1.1 · EN

## Day Two Hundred and Two · Thursday 23 April

The meeting was at two in the afternoon in a room over a bank in Ulsan that the association has used since 2006 and pays four hundred thousand won a year for.

Georgij was in Seoul, at the desk with the east window, and did not go anywhere near it.

There was no version of that room with him in it that was not the whole thing ruined. He had known that since the fourteenth. He had still spent about four minutes on the Wednesday night finding out whether there was one.

What he did with the afternoon was the other half.

A bound catalogue went out in the first days of March to every buyer that house has. One page of it was photocopied and was on a desk in Ulsan on the fourteenth.

Between those two facts there is a machine that somebody used, and machines leave marks, and the marks are not on the page.

The house uses a mailing bureau in Mapo, because a firm of that size does not put six hundred parcels in the post itself and has not since the nineties.

A bureau of that kind runs on a spreadsheet with an address list in it, and it keeps the list, because the client always comes back next season and nobody wants to key it again.

He had that list at about eleven on the Thursday morning.

It took him a little over two hours and he did not enjoy it as much as he had expected to, which he noted and did not examine.

Six hundred and forty names.

A hundred and nine of them are companies with no natural person on the line at all, and those he set aside. A man who is careful about four layers of ownership does not have an auction catalogue posted to a shell.

Of the rest, one is Annie. One is Mrs Sunwoo. One is a woman in Daegu who has bought porcelain there twice a year since 1991 and has never bought anything else.

And that is where it stopped, because six hundred names is not a list of suspects, it is a telephone directory.

He sat and looked at it and understood that he had been going at it from the wrong end for four days.

The right end is not who received a catalogue.

The right end is who received a catalogue and was also at a lunch given by the port authority in the first week of January.

That is a room with six men in it, and Woo gave him five of the names at a car door on the ninth of April, and he has had them in a notebook for a fortnight.

He put the five names next to six hundred and forty at about half past one.

Three of the five are on the mailing list.

Three is not an answer. Three is a shape.

All three of them are exactly the sort of man who would be at that lunch and would also buy at that house, which is another way of saying that nothing has been eliminated. What he has is that the two lists touch, and that they touch in three places and not in none.

He wrote the three on a separate page and put the six hundred and forty in a drawer, and he did not look at either again that day.

* * *

The telephone went at twenty past four.

"It is done," said Mr Kwon.

"Say it in the order it happened, and take as long as you like."

There was a pause of the sort a man makes when he has been asked for something he did not know he wanted to give.

"Six of us and her. She chaired it, because she is the chair. She opened it herself and read out the request for the meeting and my name at the bottom of it. She read it in the same voice she reads everything in."

"Go on."

"Then I asked her. I had it written down and I did not need it." He stopped. "I asked whether she had written to anybody on association letterhead in the last six months."

"And."

"She said yes."

"Straight away," said Mr Kwon. "Not a second. Not one of those things where a person takes a breath first."

"Then I asked her what was in it and she said that was hers and she was not going to say. And Gil asked whether it concerned the business of the association."

Georgij did not say anything.

"She said no."

"That is all she said," said Mr Kwon. "One word. She could have said it was association business and none of us would have been able to prove otherwise for a month, and by then it would have been over. She had a way out and it was standing open and she looked straight at it and said no."

"She does not lie."

"She does not lie. I have known that for twenty years and I have never once had it cost me anything." He said that part quite slowly. "It cost her everything in about a second and a half and she did not even look surprised."

The room over the bank in Ulsan did the thing rooms do.

"After that it was not really a discussion," said Mr Kwon. "Somebody has to say the words, so I said them. That she had used the one thing the six of us own together, for something that was not ours, and had not told us for five weeks."

"And she said."

"She said that was correct."

"Four for. One against. And Cho abstained."

"Cho is the man of forty," said Georgij.

"Cho is the man of forty who has not said one word at a meeting since October, and he said one today." Mr Kwon's voice did something. "He said that she had written to six people on the first of every month for twenty years, and that nobody in that room had ever once written back." Mr Kwon stopped. "And that he did not think a person should lose everything for the first thing she ever asked for herself."

"And then he abstained."

"And then he abstained. He said the words and then he put his hand down and did not vote. I have been thinking about it for two hours and I cannot decide whether it was the most honest thing I have ever seen a man do or the most useless."

"Both," said Georgij. "They are not in competition."

He did not say the rest of it.

A man who says a true thing in a room and then does not act on it has made himself feel better and changed nothing whatever. It is the commonest thing in the world. He has used it in nine rooms since October and has never once had to work for it.

"Who is chair," he said.

"Me. I did not want it and there was nobody else and it took four minutes."

"And her firm."

"Her firm is a member. It is still a member. Nobody moved to expel her and I would have voted against it if they had." Mr Kwon said it flatly. "She has four trucks and one lane and no work on it. The rest of us are about to have seven years. Everybody in that room understood exactly what that means, and not one of us said it out loud."

"No," said Georgij. "They never do."

There was a longer pause after that.

"You were not there," said Mr Kwon.

"No."

"You were not there and it took forty minutes and every single thing that happened in that room happened because a man came to my yard on a Tuesday and said two words to me."

"Three, if you count the first one."

"Do not do that." It came out harder than the rest of it. "I have got nine trucks and a partner who can go and live near his daughter. I am going to sign that paper on Monday and be pleased about it for about a year. Do not stand at the other end of a telephone and be clever at me about it."

"I am sorry," said Georgij.

He does not say that often, and he had not planned to, and it came out before he had decided whether it was for Mr Kwon or for himself, which is the reason he almost never says it.

"What did she do afterwards," he said.

"She stayed and helped stack the chairs. It is what she has done after every meeting for twenty years and I do not think it occurred to her to do anything else." Mr Kwon stopped. "Then she said goodnight to each of us by name, and she said mine last, and she has never done that before, and she was not being pointed. I do not think she is capable of being pointed."

"Thank you," said Georgij.

"I do not want to be thanked by you."

"No."

"I will take the seven years and I will not take the thanks, and I would like that to be the arrangement between us from now on."

"That is the arrangement."

He put the telephone down and sat with the chairs for a while, which he had not intended to do.

A woman loses everything at two in the afternoon and then stays behind and stacks the chairs, because it is a Thursday and that is what happens after a meeting, and there is no procedure anywhere for stopping.

He has watched a great many people take bad news since October and he can predict most of what they do with the first ten minutes of it.

He did not predict that, and he cannot make it be anything other than what it is, and it does not alter one single thing about the rest of the month.

He wrote the date in the notebook after that, and underneath it, in five words: *four for, one against, one abstained.*

He did not write down what Cho said. He could have, and he can still remember the whole of it.

A thing that goes in the notebook stops having to be carried. That is the entire point of the notebook and he has known it since October, and he left it out anyway.

He went down at about seven.

Annie was in the small room and the door was open and she looked up when he came into it, and he had not been into that room to say anything since the third of April.

"It is not finished," he said. "That was the first half and it took twenty days."

"I know. I have had the Yeouido people watching the filings since Tuesday and I did not tell you, because you would have wanted to know why and the answer is that I wanted to see it happen." She put down what she was holding. "Sit down and do not report to me. You do not have to and I have not asked."

He sat down on the floor at the side of the desk, which is where he sits.

"There is a man called Cho," he said, "and he is forty, and he said the only true thing in that room, and then he put his hand down and did not vote."

Annie did not answer for a moment.

"Do you want me to say something about that."

"No, Mistress."

"Then I will not," she said, and went back to what she was doing, and left her hand where it was for the next hour.

---

# Book Two · Chapter 16: A hand
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 1.2 · EN

## Day Two Hundred and Four · Saturday 25 April

The house on the river stopped taking new consignments on the twentieth of April.

There was no announcement, because a firm of that kind does not announce anything. It tells the fourth person who telephones that the spring sale is under review, and by the eighth person it has stopped being a rumour.

Mrs Sunwoo has had a dozen lunches since the twenty-fourth of March.

Georgij knows the number because it is in a diary that a woman of eighty-one has never in her life hidden from anybody. He has looked at it twice and both times felt something he decided not to name.

Two of the people she has eaten with are on the mailing list, and one of those two is on the port authority's January list as well. He did not do anything with that. A coincidence in a city of that size is not a fact, and a man who forgets that ends up with the wrong person.

Three names, and two of them came off on the Friday evening without anything clever being done to them at all.

The first is a man who owns two feeder vessels and has been trying to buy small operators on that coast for six years, and everybody in the trade knows it, including the small operators. He asks about lanes the way a man with a bag asks about houses. If he had put a page in front of Nam Byung-hee she would have telephoned four people about it before dinner.

The second sits on the port authority's own committee and has done since 2014, which means he was at that lunch because it is his lunch. A man who is at a thing because it belongs to him does not use it to ask a question he wants nobody to remember.

The third is Mr Ok.

He is fifty-six. He runs a marine insurance brokerage that his father ran before him, from two floors of a building in Jung-gu that the family owns and does not let. Eleven staff. He has bought at that house since 2003, mostly ceramics, occasionally a scroll, never anything anybody would talk about.

He has no interest in coastal feeder capacity. He does not broker it, insure much of it, or own any of it. There is no lane in this country that his business would be better or worse off for.

He was at the lunch because somebody at the port authority puts the same forty people on the same list every January, and he goes because his father went.

That is the whole of it and it took Georgij about an hour to establish, and it is not evidence of anything.

A man is allowed to ask a question at a lunch.

What he did on the Saturday morning is the thing he has not done properly since October, and he had been putting it off for a week without admitting that was what he was doing.

He is not going to write down how, and it does not matter.

A brokerage of eleven people that has been in the same two floors since 1978 keeps everything in one place, because that is what a firm does when nobody has ever forced it to think about the question. There is a machine in the corridor that scans, copies and sends. It has been there since 2019. It keeps a log, because everything of that kind keeps a log, and nobody in that building has ever once looked at it.

It took him fifty minutes and most of that was reading.

The catalogue came into that office on the third of March, because the mailing bureau's list has the office address on it and not the house.

On the twelfth of March at four forty in the afternoon, one page was put on the glass of the machine in the corridor and copied twice.

The log says the page was copied. It does not say what happened to the copies after that, because a machine in a corridor does not follow paper out of a building.

Somebody put one of them in an envelope. That is in no log anywhere and it never will be, and it is the reason a page arrives in Ulsan and a man in Jung-gu has no idea that he has done anything at all.

Twice.

He sat with that for a moment, because two copies is a fact and one copy is an errand.

The account the machine was signed in under belongs to a woman who has done the filing in that office since 2004.

She copied it at four forty on a Thursday, in a corridor, on the machine everybody uses. She did not think about it for one second. A person who is asked to copy a page copies the page.

Georgij went back through the log for six months either side and found nothing else that was not invoices, survey reports and one birthday card.

Then he went and looked at what else that office holds, which took the rest of the morning, and the finding is an absence and it is the whole chapter.

There is no instruction anywhere.

No message, no note, no letter, no entry in a diary. Nothing on the eleventh, nothing on the tenth, nothing in the week before it. There is no lunch in the calendar that is not one of the four he has every month with the same three men. There is no telephone number in that building that received a call it should not have.

The page went on the glass at four forty on the twelfth of March and nothing at all happened in that office before it.

Georgij sat back from the desk at about half past one on the Saturday and understood what he had.

In the small room on the third of April he had said it out loud to Annie, and she had made him say it twice.

*A man who is carrying a thing gets rid of it on the day he is told to. Somebody sat down with a calendar. Nobody sits down with a calendar on somebody else's behalf.*

There is no calendar in that office.

Mr Ok did not choose the week. Mr Ok did not choose the page and did not choose Nam Byung-hee and almost certainly could not tell you today what an association chair is. Somebody asked him at a lunch in January whether he knew that coast, and he said what a man says. Nine weeks later somebody asked him for a favour that cost a woman in his corridor about four minutes.

He is a hand.

Georgij sat with that for a long time and did not enjoy any of it.

He had wanted a man to take apart. He had said so in that room, in front of her, in the flattest voice he owns, and he had been carrying it since the eighteenth of March, and it had been the shape of his whole month.

What he has is a broker of fifty-six who buys ceramics and does what he is asked by somebody at a lunch.

He wrote it down at about two, because a thing that is not written down has to be carried and he has been carrying enough.

*Ok. A hand. Nothing before the twelfth. Frightened only.*

He looked at the last two words for a while and then did not change them.

That is his own rule and he made it in front of her before he knew who it was going to protect, which is the only condition under which a rule is worth anything at all.

If he takes that man apart now, he is a man who said a thing in a room and meant it right up until it was inconvenient. He has watched forty people do that since October and he has used every single one of them.

There is a second thing in it and it took him until about three to see it, and it is worse than the first.

Two copies.

A page that is copied twice at four forty on a Thursday afternoon is a page that goes to two places.

One of them was on a desk in Ulsan on the fourteenth.

And a man who makes two copies of a thing is a man who does not expect the first one to be enough.

Whatever he thought he was setting off in Ulsan, he had already decided in March that she might not do it, and that it would not particularly matter if she did not.

He does not know where the other one went.

He does not know whether it has been used or is going to be. It may be sitting in a drawer in a house somewhere in this country, belonging to somebody who has been waiting since March for a reason to open it.

That is the first thing in eight weeks that has actually frightened him.

* * *

He went down at about six and stood in the doorway of the small room, and Annie looked up and saw his face and put down what she was holding before he had said anything at all.

"Say the bad one first," she said.

"There is a second copy," said Georgij. "It was made on the same afternoon on the same machine and I do not know where it is."

Annie did not move for a moment.

"And the good one."

"There is no good one. There is a smaller one." He came into the room. "I have the man who made the copies. He is a broker in Jung-gu who buys porcelain and he did not choose anything. There is no instruction in that building anywhere, and there is nothing in his calendar, and by my own test he is a hand."

"By your own test that you gave me on the third of April."

"Yes."

"Say what you are going to do about him."

"Frighten him," said Georgij. "Nothing else. Not his firm, not his family and not one won."

Annie looked at him for a while.

"You have wanted somebody for five weeks."

"I have wanted somebody for five weeks and I have got a man who copies pages in a corridor." He did not soften it. "If I take him apart because I have been carrying something since March, then I am doing it for me, and I told you on the third of April that I would tell you the truth about that afterwards. This is the afterwards arriving early."

She did not say anything for a moment.

"Then that is the second thing you have brought me in a month that I did not have to ask for," said Annie. "Sit down. Tell me about the second copy from the beginning, and do not leave out the part where you are frightened, because I would rather have it from you than watch you carry it around the house for a fortnight."

He sat down on the floor at the side of the desk, which is where he sits, and told her the whole of it, including that part.

And somewhere in the middle of it, without stopping, he understood what the second copy actually means, and he did not say it out loud until he had gone through it twice.

The man who chose the week did not want one page delivered.

He wanted two, from a machine in a corridor in a building he has never entered, by a woman who will never be able to say who asked her, on a Thursday afternoon in March.

One of them went to a woman in Ulsan who was drowning and who does not lie.

The other one is somewhere, and it has been somewhere for six weeks, and it did not have to be used yet.

---

# Book Two · Chapter 17: Four minutes of somebody's time
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 1.1 · EN

## Day Two Hundred and Six · Monday 27 April

Mr Ok leaves his building at twenty past six and walks to the corner and waits for a car that is always eight minutes late. The driver takes his own mother somewhere at six and has done for four years, and Mr Ok has never once mentioned it.

That is the second most useful thing Georgij learned about him and he learned it on the Sunday.

He was on the corner at ten past.

"Mr Ok."

"Yes."

"My name will not mean anything to you. I would like four minutes and I am not selling anything and I am not from a newspaper."

Mr Ok looked at him the way a man of that sort looks at a stranger who knows his name, which is politely and with the whole of his attention.

"Four minutes," he said. "My car is eight minutes late."

"I know. That is why I am here now."

There is a low wall outside that building with a hedge behind it, and they stood at it. Mr Ok put his case down on it the way a man does when he expects to pick it up again shortly.

"On the twelfth of March," said Georgij, "at twenty to five in the afternoon, a page was copied twice on the machine in your corridor."

Mr Ok did not say anything.

"It was the last page of the tidied catalogue that the auction house on the river sent to its buyers in the first week of March. Yours came into the office on the third. It has four references on it and a phrase underneath them."

"I am not going to discuss my office with a man on a wall."

"No. I am not asking you to discuss anything. I am going to tell you what happened and then I am going to ask you one question, and you can decide about the question when we get there."

Mr Ok picked his case back up.

"Then be quick."

"Mrs Uhm did the copying," said Georgij. "She has done your filing since 2004 and she did not think about it for one second, because a person who is asked to copy a page copies the page. She is not going to be troubled by anybody, ever, and I want that said before the rest of it."

That is the sentence that stopped him, and Georgij had known on the Sunday that it would be, and had put it there for that reason and had also meant it.

"One of those copies went to a woman in Ulsan called Nam Byung-hee. She runs four trucks and she chaired an association of six small shippers for twenty years." He said it evenly and did not hurry. "On the twenty-third of April her six members voted her out of the chair. Her lane has no work on it. By the middle of May she will not have a firm."

The traffic did what traffic does.

"That has nothing to do with me," said Mr Ok.

"No," said Georgij. "It has nothing to do with you at all, and that is the exact truth, and I am not going to improve it."

"Then why are you standing at my wall."

"Because it went through your machine," he said. "It did not go through anybody else's. Somebody asked you for something small, in a way that made it small, and it was small, and it is the reason a woman of fifty-eight stayed behind on Thursday and stacked the chairs."

Mr Ok put the case down again.

He did not do it deliberately. That is simply where his hand went.

"I want to be clear about what is not going to happen," said Georgij, "because you are about to spend the night working out what it is, and you will get it wrong, and I would rather you slept."

"Say it, then."

"Nothing is going to happen to your firm. Nothing is going to happen to your family. Nobody is going to speak to your clients, your insurers, your bank or your brother-in-law in Suwon." He did not soften it. "You will not lose one won over this and neither will anybody who works for you. That is not a negotiating position and there is nothing you can do that would change it."

Mr Ok looked at him for a long moment.

"Why."

"Because you did not choose anything." Georgij kept his hands where they could be seen. "You were asked and you said yes, the way a man says yes to a small thing, and I do not take people apart for that. It is the only rule I have made this month and I made it before I knew it was going to be about you."

"You are telling me I am not worth it."

"I am telling you that you are a hand."

That landed harder than any of the rest of it, and Georgij watched it land and did not enjoy it and did not look away either.

"Now the question," he said.

"Who asked you."

Mr Ok did not answer for about six seconds.

"I do not know his name."

"Say the whole of it and let me decide what it is worth."

"There was a lunch in January. The port authority does one every year and puts the same forty people on the list, and I go because my father went and because there is nobody left who remembers that he did." He said it looking at the road. "A man I had not met before came and sat down beside me at the end of it. Somebody introduced us and I did not catch it and I did not ask, because you do not, and afterwards I thought he had said his own name and I had missed it."

"What did he ask you."

"Whether I had ever done business on the Ulsan coast, and what the small operators there were like, and then he asked about one of them by name."

Georgij did not move at all.

"He gave you the name."

"He gave me the name. I said I did not know her and that everybody says she is honest and that her lane is too small for anything." Mr Ok stopped. "He was very interested in the word honest. I remember that because I thought at the time that it was an odd thing to be interested in."

"And in March."

"He telephoned. In the first week. He said we had met at the lunch, which we had, and he asked whether I received the catalogues from that house and I said yes." He was speaking more slowly now, the way a man does when he is walking backwards through something and finding out how it looks. "He said there was a page in the back that a friend of his in Ulsan had been asking about, and would I have somebody copy it and send it down. He gave me the address. It was four minutes of somebody's time."

"Two copies."

"He asked for two. He said one to send and one for the file." Mr Ok's voice did something on that. "I have thought about a dozen things since you started talking and that is the one I did not think about at all until now."

"What did you do with the second one."

"I put it in the tray for Mrs Uhm to file and I never saw it again, and it will be in a folder somewhere on the second floor and I could probably find it in twenty minutes."

Georgij looked at him.

"Then that is not the one I am afraid of."

"What did he sound like."

"Ordinary," said Mr Ok. "Sixty, perhaps a little less. Not tall. He did not eat anything at that lunch, not one thing, and I noticed it because my mother would have made a remark. Pleasant. Quiet. The sort of man who has been in rooms."

"And at the end."

"At the end of the lunch he stood up and shook my hand and said that he was glad we had finally met."

Georgij stood on a pavement in Jung-gu at twenty-six minutes past six and did not say anything for about four seconds.

"Finally."

"Finally. We had never met. I have thought about it two or three times since and I decided he had confused me with somebody, and I put it down and I have not taken it up again until this minute."

"Mr Ok."

"Yes."

"He did not confuse you with anybody."

The car came, and it was eight minutes late.

"Is there anything I should do?" said Mr Ok.

"No. Nothing at all. Go home." Georgij stepped back from the wall. "If he telephones you again, say yes to whatever it is, and do it exactly as he asks, and do not warn anybody and do not refuse him."

"That is not what I expected you to say."

"I know. It is what I am saying."

Mr Ok stood with his hand on the door of his car.

"That woman in Ulsan," he said. "Is there anything at all."

"No," said Georgij. "There is nothing, and I would like you to hear me say that rather than spend a year deciding it for yourself. It is finished and it cannot be unfinished and you could not have prevented it in March, because you did not know one single thing."

"But it went through my corridor."

"It went through your corridor."

Mr Ok got into the car and did not close the door for a moment.

"You said your name would not mean anything to me."

"It will not."

"I should like it anyway."

Georgij told him.

It meant nothing to him, exactly as it had at a gate in Ulsan in March, and it did not do the thing this time that it had done then.

He noticed on the way to the bridge what he had done with the last thirty seconds of it.

Somebody telephoned that man in March and asked him for four minutes of somebody else's time, and he said yes, because it was small.

And a man stood at his wall this evening and told him that if the telephone goes again he is to say yes, and to do it exactly as it is asked, and to warn nobody.

There is a word for that and he did not go looking for it.

He wrote it in the notebook in the back of his own car before they were out of Jung-gu, and it came to nine lines.

Sixty or a little less. Not tall. Ate nothing. Pleasant, quiet, has been in rooms. Was introduced in a way that did not carry a name. Telephoned in the first week of March, six weeks after a lunch, and knew what post that office receives.

And at the end of it, in his own writing, the thing he had known since the pavement:

*I am glad we finally met.*

That is not a description. Any of four hundred men in this city is sixty and quiet and not tall.

But it is a sentence, and a sentence belongs to somebody, and there is one person alive who has been in enough rooms in this city to hear a sentence and put a face on it.

She has been declining to give him that face since December, and she has had a reason every single time, and every one of those reasons was better in December than it is tonight.

---

# Book Two · Chapter 18: The face on it
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 1.2 · EN

## Day Two Hundred and Six · Monday 27 April

He came in at about half past seven with the notebook and did not sit down on the floor.

"I have nine lines," he said. "Eight of them are worth nothing. I am going to read you all nine anyway, because if I read you the one you will hear it differently."

"Read them."

He read them.

Sixty or a little less. Not tall. Ate nothing at a lunch, not one thing. Pleasant, quiet, the sort of man who has been in rooms. Introduced in a way that did not carry a name. Telephoned in the first week of March. Knew what post that office receives. Asked for two copies, one to send and one for the file. Was extremely interested in the word honest.

And then the ninth.

"At the end of the lunch he stood up and shook Mr Ok's hand and said that he was glad they had finally met."

"They had never met before," said Georgij.

Annie was standing at the window when he began and she was still standing at it when he finished, and nothing whatever happened to her face.

That is not the same as nothing happening.

He has watched her hear four hundred things in this room since October. He knows the six or seven small things that occur when she is surprised, when she is bored, when she has already had it from somebody else that afternoon and is letting him say it anyway.

None of the six or seven happened.

She went entirely still in the way that a person does not go still by accident, and she held it for about two seconds too long, and then she turned round.

"You know him," said Georgij.

"Yes."

There it was, and it was the first time she had ever answered that question with a word.

"Since when."

"Long before you. That is the only part of it that is not going to be useful to you." She came away from the window. "Ask me the next one properly, because you have been building up to it since the fourteenth of March and I want it in a full sentence."

"Will you give me his name?"

"No."

Georgij put the notebook down on the desk.

"Then I am going to argue with you about it," he said, "and I would like to say before I start that I know I am going to lose, and that I am doing it anyway."

"Do it anyway."

"In December it was that I was better without it, and you were right and it worked and I have said so." He kept his hands at his sides. "In March it was that I would get it exactly right, and that getting it exactly right in front of a man who reads arrangements is the same as signing my name. That was also right and I have behaved as though it was."

"Go on."

"Both of those were about protecting the work." He did not hurry any of it. "There is no work left to protect. He has spent me. He put a page in front of a woman in Ulsan in March, and I have spent six weeks taking her apart with my own hands, and I have made a very great deal of noise doing it. Whatever I was on the second of March, I am not it now."

Annie did not interrupt him.

"And there is a second half," said Georgij. "It is not an argument and I am going to say it anyway."

"Say it."

"On Thursday I am going to a yard in Ulsan for the third time. She has asked one question since January and it is the same question every month for twenty years, which is whether anybody is going to answer her." He looked at her. "I can give her the whole of it except one word. And the one word is the only part she would actually want."

The clock in the hall did the thing it does.

"That is the worst argument you have ever made in this room," said Annie, "and it is the first one that has ever been difficult to listen to, and I want you to understand that those are two different sentences."

"I know."

She sat down at the desk.

"Now I am going to answer you, and I am going to do it properly, because you have earned that and because I am not going to hide behind December for the rest of my life."

"He does not act," she said. "Not once, not ever, not in twenty-five years. He has never given an instruction that anybody could repeat, and he has never asked anybody for anything they could refuse. What he does is put two people in the same room who each have half of something, and then he goes home."

"I know that. I worked that out on a bridge on the ninth of April."

"You worked out that he does it. You have not worked out what it costs him." She put both hands flat. "It costs him nothing at all. He can do it eleven times a year for the rest of his life and there is no version of it that is ever illegal, ever provable or ever even impolite. That is not a man with a method. That is a man who has found a way to live."

"And the name."

"The name changes exactly one thing," said Annie, "and it is not what you think it is. It does not tell you where he is, because he is at four addresses and none of them is his. It does not tell you what he owns, because he owns nothing that is in his own name and neither do you and you of all people should be able to see the shape of that."

Georgij did not say anything for a moment.

"What it changes is you."

"Say it."

"You have walked past him twice," said Annie. "Once in October and once in March, and both times you did the correct thing, and both times the correct thing was invisible because you did not know he was there. The day you have his name, everything you do about him is a decision. You will not go at a company because it is his. You will be careful at a dinner because he might be at it. You will read a guest list differently." She did not raise her voice at any point. "And he reads guest lists for a living."

"So the name is the thing that makes me legible."

"The name is the thing that makes you legible. It is the only thing in this that has ever been able to. You have spent eight weeks being the one man in Seoul he cannot see, and it has been entirely accidental." She let that sit. "I am not going to spend it on a Thursday afternoon in a yard in Ulsan so that a woman who tried to blackmail me can feel answered."

Georgij stood there and did not have anything.

"That is the argument," said Annie. "It is better than yours and we both know it, and I am sorry, and being sorry does not move it."

He heard the word and did not react to it, because reacting to it would have made her regret saying it, and she says it perhaps twice a year.

"May I have one thing instead?" he said.

"Ask."

"Not the name. One true thing about him that I can use, that does not tell me where he is or what he owns."

Annie thought about that for a while, and it was a real pause and not a performance.

"He eats," she said.

Georgij looked up.

"He eats a great deal, and he enjoys it, and he does it at his own table and nowhere else." She said it very precisely. "He does not eat in front of people he is working on. It is not a discipline and it is not a superstition, it is simply that he cannot do both at once, and he has never in his life noticed that it shows."

"That is not one true thing," said Georgij. "That is a rule about a room."

"Yes."

"It means that Mr Ok was being worked on at a lunch in January and did not know it."

"It means rather more than that." Annie picked up the notebook and turned it round and read the ninth line again. "It means that every man in this city who has ever sat down to a meal with him and watched him eat is a man he was not working on. And I can think of five of those and you have met two of them."

Georgij stood absolutely still.

"You are not going to tell me which two."

"No. You will get there in about four days and it will be yours when you do." She put the notebook down. "That is the difference between a name and a thing you can use, and I have just given you the second one, and it is worth more and you know it is."

He went at it on the stairs afterwards and did not get anywhere, which is correct. A thing a man gets on a staircase is a thing he would have got sitting down.

Two men he has eaten with, out of everybody he has eaten with since October. There have been nine of those meals and he can name every one of them without looking at anything.

He did not write the list tonight. He will write it on Wednesday, on paper, with dates, in the order the meals happened, because that is how a list stops being a feeling.

And one of the nine was the eighteenth of November, and he already knows what he is going to find when he gets to it, and he is going to write it down in its place anyway.

He picked up the notebook and did not open it.

"Thank you, Mistress."

"Do not thank me for that. I have been sitting on the whole of it since December and I have just given you the smallest piece I could get away with, and both of us are aware of it."

"Yes."

"Say it, then. You are permitted to."

"You are still holding it," said Georgij.

"I am still holding it."

"And on the day you stop holding it, it will be because it has stopped being safe and not because I asked."

"Yes."

She looked at him.

"Does that make you angry."

There it was, and it is the first question she has ever asked him that he had to think about before he could answer it.

"No," he said, in the end. "It makes me tired, and I have not been tired since the second of March, and I would rather be angry. Anger is quicker."

Annie did not say anything to that for a moment.

Then she got up and came round the desk and stood in front of him. She put her hand flat on the side of his neck above the collar, which she has done perhaps three times since October and never once in that room.

"Go to bed," she said. "You are going to Ulsan on Thursday and you are not going to say one untrue thing to that woman. You are going to manage it because you have not got the name and cannot give it to her."

"That is not why you kept it."

"No," said Annie. "But it is going to be true on Thursday anyway."

---

# Book Two · Chapter 19: The third time
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 1.2 · EN

## Day Two Hundred and Nine · Thursday 30 April

He wrote the list on the Wednesday morning as he had said he would, on paper, with dates, in the order the meals happened.

Nine of them. The thirty-first of October, the eighteenth of November, the sixteenth of December, and six others.

Then he looked at it for a while and put a line through the whole page.

Annie had said *met*, and he had heard *eaten with*, and he had heard it because a list of nine meals is a thing a man can do in an hour at his own desk.

The set of people he has met since October is not nine. It is somewhere over four hundred, and the great majority of them are a name on a docket and a handshake in a room with three hundred people in it.

That is the whole of Wednesday's work and it produced nothing except an accurate view of how much there is.

There is exactly one man he could ask who would know and would answer and would want nothing for it.

He did not ask him, because a man who telephones Park Sang-hoon to ask which people have watched a particular person eat has just told Park Sang-hoon a great deal, and Park Sang-hoon does not put things down.

The house on the river closed to the public on the twenty-ninth of April, which is a Wednesday and which nobody outside the trade noticed at all.

There was no notice on the door. There is a telephone number, answered by a woman who says that the spring sale has been postponed and that she is not able to say more than that. She has been saying it since the twentieth.

Mrs Sunwoo has three more lunches in the diary for May.

Mrs Seo left the envelope on the table outside his room on the Wednesday night without being asked, and there was a third thing in it this time.

Two tickets and the card with the platform, and a small paper bag with two of the rice cakes that the boy makes on Wednesdays.

She did not mention it in the morning and neither did he.

* * *

The yard on the thirtieth of April has two trucks in it and room for nine.

The other two went on the twenty-third and the twenty-fourth, to a dealer in Busan, at a price she will have accepted in about ninety seconds because there was nothing else on the table.

She came out before he was through the gate.

"Third time," said Nam Byung-hee.

"Yes."

"You have not brought anything."

"No."

"Then come up."

The office over the weighbridge has the same calendar on the wall and there is now nothing written on it at all, in any month.

She sat down first this time and told him to sit, which she did not do in March.

"I am going to say a number of things," said Georgij, "and none of them is going to help you, and I am going to say them anyway, and then I am going to answer whatever you ask me. That is the whole of what this is."

"Then say them."

"You were unlucky about the containers." He did not decorate any of it. "That clause was never aimed at you. Nobody in Seoul had ever heard your name when it was drafted and nobody thought about your lane for one second. There was no way for you to see it and there was nothing you could have done about it."

"You were unlucky about the envelope. In January a man in Seoul was asking about the small operators on this coast. He asked about you by name, and the word he was interested in was honest." He did not look away from her. "That is what you were chosen for. There was no way for you to see that either."

Nam Byung-hee did not say anything.

"And at the end you were unlucky in the way you thought," said Georgij. "On one afternoon in March, after four days, you decided to put a hand on the woman I belong to."

He stopped there for a moment.

"That part was yours. It is the only part of the whole of it that was, and it is the reason for everything that has happened to you since, and I am not going to pretend it is bad luck as well."

"No," she said. "Do not."

The gulls did whatever gulls do over a yard with two trucks in it.

"Now the part that is mine," said Georgij.

"On the eighteenth of March, in the hour your letter came out of the envelope, I decided what I was going to do to you."

She looked up at that.

"I did not admit that to myself until the twentieth. I came down here on the twentieth and told you the truth about the veto, and you stood down. Then I got back on the train and wrote something in a notebook about a woman who is not drowning any more staying where she has been put." He said it perfectly evenly. "And I did not put it on either side of the account. I wrote it in the middle of the page as a thing I had not decided about. I had decided about it. I have known that since the twenty-third of April."

"So that is the part I am apologising for," he said. "Not the rest of it. The twentieth of March, and letting you thank me for it, and knowing while you did."

Nam Byung-hee sat and looked at the desk for a while.

"You could have got out of that," she said. "Nobody in the world would have known. I would not have known. You have just handed me the one piece of it that you did not have to hand anybody."

"Yes."

"Why."

"Because I said I would not tell you one untrue thing, and leaving that out is the only untrue thing available in this room."

She did not accept it and she did not reject it. She put it somewhere and went on.

"Two things you should know," said Georgij. "They are both true and neither of them is a comfort, and I would like you to have them anyway."

"Say them."

"Your nephew started at Mr Kwon's on the twentieth of April. He is on the nine-truck rota, he is on a proper wage for the first time, and he will still be there in ten years if he wants to be." He did not soften it. "That was arranged and it was arranged by me, and I checked on Monday that it had actually happened, because I do not take anybody's word for a thing I have said I would do."

Nam Byung-hee put one hand flat on the desk and left it there.

"He telephoned me on the twenty-first and I could not work out why he was frightened to tell me."

"He was frightened because he thought you would think he had gone over."

"He had gone over."

"Yes," said Georgij. "And he is twenty-three, and he is not the one who wrote the letter."

She took a moment over that.

"And the second thing."

"The man who chose you is going to get exactly what you have got."

"You do not know who he is."

"No. I have nine lines of description and a sentence he said to somebody in January, and I am going to have him before the end of the year." Georgij did not raise his voice at any point. "When I do, he will lose the whole of it, and he will lose it slowly enough to watch it coming. You did not. That is the only respect in which you have been treated better than he is going to be."

"You are telling me that to make me feel better."

"I am telling you because it is true and because you asked in your letter to be answered." He looked at her. "It is the only answer I have got and it is four months late."

The room over the weighbridge did the thing rooms do.

"One question," said Nam Byung-hee.

"Ask it."

"Was it you, or was it her."

There it was, and she had waited an hour and a half of her own life to ask it, and she asked it in the same voice she reads everything in.

"It was me," said Georgij. "I asked her for it on the third of April, standing up, and she could have said no and did not. She permitted it. She did not order it, she did not suggest it, and there is no version of this in which I was doing what I was told."

"So it was personal."

"It was entirely personal."

"Why," said Nam Byung-hee. "You had never met me. I did not know your name until you wrote it on the corner of a pad in this room."

"Because the thing you picked up and pointed at her was me."

She did not understand it at first, and he watched her not understand it, and then he watched her arrive.

"Lot fourteen," she said.

"Lot fourteen."

"On the page. In my coat, for twelve days."

"Yes."

Nam Byung-hee sat back in her chair and put both hands in her lap and did not say anything for a long time.

"I have thought about that page every day since the fourteenth of March," she said at last, "and it never once occurred to me that there was a person on it."

"No. That is what a page is for."

"And the man in January knew there was."

"The man in January knew exactly whose line it was before he ever heard of you." Georgij said it flatly. "He gave you a page with a man on it and did not tell you the man was on it, and then he went home and had his dinner."

She looked out of the window at two trucks.

"Then I am going to say something and you are going to let me finish it," she said.

"Yes."

"I am not sorry that I wrote it. I have tried for a week to be and I am not." She was quite steady. "I had forty-one people and no work and nobody would tell me why, and I wrote one letter, and I would write it again tomorrow if I did not know what I know now. That is the truth and I am not going to improve it for you."

"No," said Georgij. "I would not have believed you if you had."

"But I understand it now," she said, "which I did not on Thursday, and I would rather understand it than not, and I do not expect that to be any use to either of us."

He stood up, because there was nothing left that was both true and sayable, and he has been in that position four times in his life and twice of them have been in this room.

At the gate she said the last of it.

"You are going to tell me not to repeat any of it."

"No," said Georgij.

"You can say all of it, to anybody, for the rest of your life." He said it without any weight on it. "Nobody is going to do anything about it, and I would like you to understand why that is, because it is the only part of this you can still get wrong."

"Say it, then."

"By the end of May there will not be one firm on this coast that is not better off than it was in December, and every one of them will know exactly who it is better off because of." He did not look away from her. "A man whose yard has just been made worth something does not repeat a thing like that. He does not decide not to. It simply does not occur to him."

Nam Byung-hee stood in her own gate with her hands in her coat.

"And if I do it anyway."

"Then I will come here a fourth time," said Georgij, "and there will be nothing in my hands then either."

She looked at him for a while.

"That is the only thing you have said today that you enjoyed."

He thought about that on the platform for twenty minutes and on the train for four hours, and he has not got an answer to it that he is willing to write down.

---

# Book Two · Chapter 20: Cheaper than deciding
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 1.3 · EN

## Day Two Hundred and Nine · Thursday 30 April

He got in at twenty past eleven and the light in the small room was on.

That is not usual. She works late perhaps twice a week and she does not wait up for anybody. She has never once in seven months been in that room at that hour on a night when there was nothing on the desk.

There was nothing on the desk.

"Come in and shut it," said Annie.

He shut it, which is the second time that door has been shut with him inside the room.

"It is the thirtieth of April," she said.

"Yes."

"You told me on the third that it would be finished by the end of April. I made you say it again on the first, because I wanted it to be a date and not a thing a man says at midnight." She had her hands flat on the desk. "It is the thirtieth of April at twenty past eleven. Report."

He stood at the corner of the desk and did it in order and it took four minutes.

The house on the river closed to the public yesterday. Mrs Sunwoo has three more lunches in the diary and does not need any of them.

Nam Byung-hee has two trucks, no chair, no work and no association standing. Mr Kwon signed on the twenty-seventh. The other four sign Chairman Woo's paper in the first week of May, seven years, the same document as the anchor cargo.

The five halves come onto the market between the middle of May and the middle of June, five separate afternoons, and there is one buyer on that coast with money in her hand and it is not a metaphor.

Mr Ok is a hand and was frightened on Monday and nothing else has happened to him or will. The second copy went into his own filing on the twelfth of March and he can put his hand on it in twenty minutes, which is the only thing this month that has turned out smaller than it looked.

The man who chose her has nine lines of description and a sentence and no name.

"That is the month," said Georgij.

"That is the month."

Annie did not say anything about any of it, which he had expected, because she does not congratulate people and it has never once occurred to her that anybody might want her to.

"Now the other thing," she said.

"Yes."

"I am not going to ask it gently and I am not going to give you a way of answering it that lets you past. I have thought about how to put it since about six o'clock." She looked at him. "Did you enjoy it."

He did not answer straight away, and she let him not answer.

"Yes," he said. "Once. For about a second and a half, at a gate, at four o'clock this afternoon."

"Say what it was."

"She asked what would happen if she talked anyway. And I told her I would come back a fourth time and there would be nothing in my hands then either." He said it perfectly evenly. "It is true, and it was the correct thing to say, and I would say it again in that position tomorrow, and I enjoyed saying it."

Annie sat back.

"And the rest of the month."

"No. Not one minute of it." He did not soften it. "Kwon's yard, no. The telephone call on the twenty-third, no, and I said I was sorry to a man who did not want it. Mr Ok at his own wall, no. This afternoon, none of it, and I have never wanted less to be anywhere in my life than I did in that office."

"And the one second and a half."

"The one second and a half, yes."

"How do you know," said Annie.

"Because she told me." He looked at the edge of the desk. "She said it was the only thing I had said all day that I enjoyed, and she said it in the voice she reads things in." He did not look up. "Then I got on a train and had four hours and could not find an answer I was prepared to write down."

"Then she is better at this than I am."

"She is not. She had the advantage of not caring what the answer was."

Annie thought about that for a moment and let it go, and it was not agreement.

"Say the rest of what you came in here to say," she said. "You have had four hours and you have been standing there working out how to get to it since I said report."

"I would like the ceiling back."

The clock in the hall did the thing it does at the quarter.

"Say it properly," said Annie.

"On the third of April I asked you to take it off me and you did, and it was the right thing to ask for and I would ask again." He had his hands behind his back. "I am asking you to put it back on. The price before it is paid, in this room, every time, and if there is no time to ask then I tell you the same night. I would like it to start tomorrow morning."

"No," said Annie.

He did not move.

"Say why," he said, "because you have got a reason and I would rather have it than the word."

"Because you are not asking me for a ceiling." She said it without any heat at all. "You are asking me to be the thing that stops you, and a man who has somebody to stop him has to decide once, on a Thursday in April, and never again."

"That is not what I am doing."

"It is exactly what you are doing and you know the shape of it better than anybody alive, because it is what you have done to eleven people since October." She did not raise her voice. "You put a rule in front of a man so that he does not have to make the decision again in June. It works every single time, and it works because a rule is cheaper than deciding."

Georgij stood there and did not have anything at all.

"I am not going to be your conscience," said Annie. "Not because I do not want the job. Because I would be extremely good at it and you would never have to do it again. In four years you would be somebody who does whatever is in front of him and comes home and has it audited."

"And if I get it wrong."

"Then you will get it wrong, and you will find out, and it will cost somebody something." She held his eye. "That is what it costs to be the person deciding. You have had two months of it now and I am not going to take it off you because the second month was unpleasant."

He did not say anything for a while.

"You are going to have to do it every time," she said. "Every single time, on the afternoon, in the room, with nobody in it but you. And there will be no version where you get to have decided already."

"That is worse than the ceiling."

"Yes. It is also the only one of the two that is worth anything, and you would have told anybody else exactly that."

He looked at the desk for a moment.

"May I say one thing that is not an argument?"

"Say it."

"I have not been afraid of very much since October," said Georgij. "I have been afraid of four things and I could name all four. As of this afternoon there are five, and the fifth one is that I am going to be all right about it."

Annie did not answer that for a moment.

"Yes," she said. "That is the correct one to be afraid of, and I would have been disappointed in you if it had taken you until June."

She got up and came round the desk.

"Sit down on the floor."

He sat down at the side of the desk with his back against it, which is where he sits. She sat on the arm of the reading chair, which is not sitting. Neither of them said anything for a while.

"What happens to her now," said Annie.

She has not asked one question about that woman since the third of April, and he had stopped expecting her to.

"Nothing happens to her. That is the whole of it." He did not soften it. "Two trucks, no work, and a name that is finished on that coast. Nobody on it will employ her, because employing her is a statement, and nobody makes a statement about a woman who has just been put out by her own six."

"And she is fifty-eight."

"She is fifty-eight, and she is the most competent person I have sat in a room with since December, and I am saying that as a piece of information and not as a sentiment."

Annie looked at the window.

"Then somebody should employ her."

"Yes," said Georgij. "In about four months. Not by me, and she is never going to know where it came from."

"Say the reasons and put them in the order they actually go in."

"Three of them." He set them out. "She is good and she is going to be wasted, and that is the one I would put last if I am being honest about the order. She is honest, and there is going to be very little of that on that coast by June."

"And the first."

"Somebody who has been armed once can be armed again." He did not look away. "A woman with nothing is a woman who says yes to the next man who arrives with a page, and I have spent six weeks finding out how cheap that is to arrange."

"That is the one that matters," said Annie.

"That is the one that matters. It closes the last hole in the whole of it."

"And there is a fourth and you have not said it."

"There is a fourth and I would sooner it were not there."

"Say it anyway."

"The man who chose her is going to hear about it." Georgij said it flatly. "He will hear that his instrument was taken apart in six weeks and then picked up off the floor by the same hand, and there is nothing at all he can do with that except sit with it."

"And why four months," said Annie.

"Because in May it is a payment. She would take it, and it would be the last thing that ever happened to her with my hand on it, and I would have bought my way out of this afternoon for the price of a job." He did not move. "She said something to me at a gate today that I have not got an answer to. I am not going to answer it with a favour."

"There is one more thing," she said eventually, "and it is not about any of that."

"Mistress."

"You have not slept properly since the eighteenth of March, and neither has anybody in this house who has been paying attention. That is Mrs Seo and Jang and me, and Mr Ku, who is the only one of the four to have said anything about it."

"He has not said anything about it."

"He said something to you on an expressway on the fourteenth of April about a yard in Gimhae." She was looking at the window and not at him. "He came and told Mrs Seo that he had said it and that he was not sure whether he should have. He has been worrying about it for a fortnight."

Georgij put his head back against the side of the desk.

"Tell him it was the most useful thing anybody said to me all month."

"Tell him yourself. He is on the early rota."

"Yes, Mistress."

She put her hand down without looking away from the window and left it in his hair, and after a while his shoulders came down about an inch, and he had known this time that they were up.

---

# Book Two · Chapter 21: Tidily
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 1.4 · EN

## Day Two Hundred and Thirteen · Monday 4 May

The front door of the house on the river is not locked, because a building that is winding up has men in it moving things and a locked door is a nuisance.

There is nobody at the desk where the visitors' book was. The book is on the counter with a pen beside it and nothing written in it since the twenty-ninth of April.

Georgij wrote his name in it anyway, and the time, and left the third column empty.

That is the third time he has written his name in a book at a door in eight weeks, and the first time nobody has been standing there to watch him do it.

The lights are on in one corridor on the first floor and off everywhere else, which is a thing a building does when there is one person left in it who is not paid by the hour.

Mr Hwang was in the room at the end with three crates open and a list.

He is about fifty. There is nothing in the room but the crates, a table and a chair, and he was standing.

"We are closed," he said, without looking up. "If you are from the fund you are two days early and I have not finished."

"I am not from the fund."

He looked up then.

"Then you have walked into a building that is shut and I have about six hours of work in front of me. Say what you want in one sentence and I will tell you whether I have got time for it."

"You have four entries on the back page of a catalogue you tidied," said Georgij. "All four were sold as deletions and all four were paid for. You found that out and you left them where they were."

Mr Hwang put the list down on the table.

"Sit down," he said. "There is one chair and you may have it, and I am going to stand, because I have been standing since seven and if I sit down now I will not get up again."

Georgij did not sit down either.

"You are correct," said Mr Hwang. "I want to say that first so that we are not doing the part where you prove it to me. I found it in the second week of December, in the settlement ledgers, and it took me about an hour."

"Four fees over four years," he said. "Three of them were in that hour. The fourth is dated the nineteenth of February, ten weeks after I stopped the practice, and I did not find it until the catalogue was already at the printer." He did not soften any of it. "Two of them are the same buyer. One is a woman who has bought here since I was a boy. One is a man."

"And there is a thing about the man that does not fit, and I am going to give it to you now, because you will get there eventually and I would sooner you got there from me."

"Say it."

"He paid the fee. It is in the ledger with the other three and it is the same money in the same hand." Mr Hwang did not move. "But the lot itself never went through settlement at all. No invoice, no clearing, no line at that desk, nothing. This house let a lot walk out of the building and did not send anybody a bill for it."

"So he was not billed for the thing," said Georgij, "and he paid to have the record of it taken out."

"Yes."

"Those two do not go together."

"They do not go together at all, and I have had five months on it." Mr Hwang picked the list up and put it down again. "A man who is not billed is a man this house owes something to, or is frightened of, or belongs to. And a man in any of those three positions does not have to pay anybody a fee. He can simply say take it out."

"And he paid anyway."

"He paid anyway, on the same terms as everybody else, in cash, through the same man." He looked up. "I have never been able to make that mean anything except one thing, and I do not like it."

"Say it."

"That he did not want anybody in this building to know that he could have had it for nothing."

"And you did nothing."

"I did not delete them. That is not the same as nothing and I am not going to let you have it as the same." He said it quite steadily. "I stopped the practice on the day I found it, and it was sold once more after that, and I have put that in front of you before you asked me for it. It has not been sold since the man who sold it walked out of this building. That is the whole of what I was brought here to do. I did it in nine weeks and it took ten more before it was true."

"Say what you did about the four."

"Nothing," said Mr Hwang.

The building did what an empty building does.

"Then that is the thing I have come about," said Georgij. "Not the practice. The four."

"They paid for a service," said Mr Hwang. "The service was not performed. I have been aware of the shape of it since December and of the last of them since the end of February, and I made a decision and I have not changed it. If I delete an entry because somebody paid for it to be deleted, then I am a man who deletes entries when there is a reason, and there is always a reason."

"That is a very good sentence," said Georgij. "I would like to take it apart, and it is going to take about a minute, and you are going to be able to follow all of it."

"Then take it apart."

"The rule you were brought here to hold is that this house does not sell deletions." He kept his hands at his sides. "It is a rule about the future. It is a rule about what you accept money for from tomorrow onwards. It has nothing whatever to say about four people who paid for something last year and did not get it."

Mr Hwang did not answer.

"You had three ways out of December and every one of them was clean," said Georgij. "You could have performed the four. You could have refunded them, in full, with a letter saying why. Or you could have told each of them exactly what had been done to them and let them decide."

"Yes."

"You took none of them. You kept the money and you kept the record." He did not soften it. "That is the position the men before you were in. The only difference between you and them on the thirty-first of December is that your filing is better."

Mr Hwang picked the list up again and then put it down without looking at it.

"That is not fair," he said.

"No," said Georgij. "It is accurate. Those are different words and I chose the one I meant."

"I could not perform them without becoming the thing I was sent to stop."

"You could not perform them. You could have refunded them in an afternoon, and it would have cost this house about a tenth of what it made from them. There is not one sentence in your rule that says a house may keep money for work it did not do." Georgij did not raise his voice at any point. "You did not decline to delete four entries. You declined to give four people back a thing they had already bought, and you called that a principle."

The room was very quiet.

"You have not been running a clean house for five months," he said. "You have been running the same house tidily."

Mr Hwang stood with one hand on the edge of the table for what was probably eight seconds.

"Say the rest of it," he said. "You did not come here to give me a lecture on obligations. There is a fifth thing and you have been holding it since you came through the door."

"On the fourteenth of March one of those four entries was photocopied and sent to a woman in Ulsan who had nothing," said Georgij. "By the twenty-third of April she had lost the chair of her association. By the middle of this month she will have no firm. She is fifty-eight and she has never told an untruth in her life and she has two trucks left."

"And the page that did that," said Mr Hwang.

"Is on the back of the catalogue you built. You put the four retained entries on one page because a thing that cannot be found goes wrong later, and you were right about that too." He looked at him. "It has been posted to every buyer this house has. It went to six hundred and forty addresses in the first week of March. It cannot be collected."

Mr Hwang sat down on one of the crates, which is not a chair, and put his forearms on his knees.

"Who are you," he said.

Georgij told him, and gave him the whole of it, which is a thing he has done four times since October.

He watched the name go in and he watched what it did, and it took about two seconds, because the man in front of him has read those four entries more carefully than anybody alive.

"Lot fourteen," said Mr Hwang.

"Yes."

"The fourth of October. Struck on the nineteenth of February." He said it the way a man reads out a shipping mark. "And retained. And I retained it."

"You retained it."

Mr Hwang looked at the floor of a room with three crates in it.

"Is the house shut because of you," he said.

There it was, and it was a direct question from a man who does not ask any other kind.
