"Yes," said Georgij. "Not by me. I told one customer the truth about what had been done to her, in one sentence, in her own house, in March. She checked it in a catalogue she already had. Everything after that was hers and I have not spoken to her since."

"And you knew what she would do."

"I knew exactly what she would do. That is why I said the sentence."

Mr Hwang nodded slowly, once, and did not say anything about it at all.

"There is one more thing and it is not an argument," said Georgij. "It is a fact and you have not got to it yet, and I would rather you had it from me standing in this room than worked it out in June."

"Say it."

"You are finished."

"Not the house. You." He did not decorate any of it. "You are the most honest man in this trade and you have five months on your record and a building that died under you. Nobody in this country is going to read that charitably and nobody is going to ask. In eleven years, when somebody says your name, the sentence after it will be the name of this house."

Mr Hwang sat on the crate with his forearms on his knees and did not move.

"I did the job," he said.

"You did the job. That is the part I cannot get past, and I have been trying since the twenty-third of April." Georgij did not look away. "Nobody in the whole of this took a single won. Not you. Not me. Not her. Everybody involved did what they thought was correct and a woman of fifty-eight is going to be selling two trucks on Thursday."

Nobody said anything for a while.

"You want something," said Mr Hwang. "You have not asked for it yet and you have been in this room for twenty minutes."

"Yes."

"Then ask, because I have got about four hours left in me and I would like to spend some of them on the crates."

"The four fees," said Georgij. "Somebody in this house took them. Four times over four years. That man had a name and a position and he signed something each time."

"Yes."

"I would like it."

Mr Hwang did not answer for a moment, and it was not reluctance and it was not calculation.

"You understand that I have spent five months not being the sort of man who does that."

"I understand that you have spent five months not doing a number of things," said Georgij, "and that we have just been over what the last one cost."

That landed, and it landed properly, and Georgij watched it land.

Mr Hwang got up off the crate and went to the table and wrote on the back of the list. He wrote for about fifteen seconds, which is longer than a name.

Then he tore the strip off and held it out. He did not put it down on the table to be picked up, which is a thing a man does when he wants it clear that he handed it over.

"A name, a position, and the dates of the four," he said. "He left at the end of February, a week after the last of them went through, and I have never been able to decide whether those are two facts or one. He told the payroll office he did not want to work for a fund."

"Where is he now."

"On the second line."

Georgij looked at it.

"Thank you."

"Do not." Mr Hwang went back to the crates. "I have just done in twenty seconds the thing I refused to do for four people in five months. I did it because a man came in here and made me feel it." He did not turn round. "I would like to be quite clear with myself about what that says."

Georgij stood in a room with three crates in it and did not have an answer for that either.

At the door he stopped.

"Mr Hwang."

"Yes."

"You will not be given another house," said Georgij. "You are going to be given work by somebody eventually and it is going to be beneath you, and you are going to take it, and you will do it properly because you cannot do anything the other way."

"Is that supposed to be a kindness."

"No. It is the last true thing I have got and I have run out of the other kind."

He wrote the time in the second column of the visitors' book on the way out, because there was nobody there to do it. The book will be in a crate by Friday and nobody is ever going to read either line.

---

# Book Two · Chapter 22: I did not offer you anything
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 1.2 · EN

## Day Two Hundred and Fifteen · Wednesday 6 May

Mr Byun kept the register of that house for twenty-six years and left it at the end of February, ten weeks after the fund completed. He did not want to work for people who would ask him what he did all day.

He is sixty-eight. He lives on the eighth floor of a building in Seongdong with a view of the river that he paid about forty per cent extra for. He told Georgij about the forty per cent inside the first two minutes.

He opened the door himself and was pleased to have a visitor.

That is the part Georgij had not prepared for and it cost him about half a minute to adjust to.

"You are from the fund," said Mr Byun. "You are the fourth one and I have told the other three everything I know, which is a great deal, because I was in that building longer than any man alive."

"I am not from the fund."

"Then you are from the auditors. Sit down. Do you want tea, I am not going to make it but I can offer it."

"I am from one of the buyers," said Georgij.

He got a small pause for that and then a completely open face.

"Which one. I had two thousand of them."

"Four of them paid you to have an entry deleted," said Georgij, "and you took the money and did not do it. October of last year, February of this year, and two others going back four years."

Mr Byun sat down on his own sofa and looked at him.

He was not frightened, and he was not angry, and there was one thing that happened on his face and it was not either of those.

He was trying to remember which four.

That is the moment Georgij will not be putting down for some time.

"The February one," said Mr Byun eventually. "That was a woman. Very direct. She came four times and on the third occasion she did not bring money, and I have thought about that visit since, because she was the only one of them who frightened me."

"And the others."

"The October one is the same woman. That one was easy, that one was a withdrawal before the sale and those are nothing." He waved a hand. "The other two are further back and I am not going to pretend I have them by heart."

"One of them was Mrs Sunwoo."

"Yes." He nodded, pleased. "Yes, that is right. Three years ago. Charming woman, very old family. She sent somebody with it in an envelope and I never saw her at all."

Georgij did not say anything for a moment.

"You do not appear to be worried," he said.

"Should I be worried."

"You took money for a service four times and did not perform it."

"I took money for a service that this house had been selling since before I sat down in that chair," said Mr Byun, and he said it comfortably and without any defensiveness at all, "and I did what was done. I did not invent it. The man before me did it and the man before him. It is the only thing that house had to sell that nobody could get anywhere else."

"And the not performing it."

"Nobody was hurt," said Mr Byun.

The river did whatever the river does eight floors down.

"There is a woman in Ulsan called Nam Byung-hee," said Georgij. "She is fifty-eight. On the fourteenth of March somebody sent her a copy of the page that has those four entries on it, and she used it. By the middle of this month she will have sold four trucks and lost a firm her brother left her."

Mr Byun looked politely interested.

"That is very unfortunate. I do not know her."

"No. She does not know you either."

"Then I am not sure what you are here for."

"I am here to tell you what is going to happen," said Georgij, "and then I am going to leave, and there is nothing in the middle."

"The four buyers are going to be told." He did not raise his voice at any point. "By name. Yours. What each of them paid, which afternoon they paid it on, and that the entry is still there. One of them is the woman who frightened you and one of them is Mrs Sunwoo, and what any of them does after that is theirs and not mine."

Mr Byun did not move for about four seconds.

Then he got up off his own sofa and stood in the middle of his own sitting room with a view he paid forty per cent extra for.

"That is not necessary," he said.

"No."

"I can give it back. All four. It is not a great deal of money and I have got it."

"I know you have got it."

"Then let me give it back."

"They are still going to be told," said Georgij.

Mr Byun stood there.

"You cannot do that. I live here. My daughter-in-law's family is in this building." He had lost about eleven years off his voice. "Mrs Sunwoo knows everybody. Everybody. She will have it round this city in a fortnight and I will not be able to go to anything."

"Yes."

"What do you want?"

"I have not asked you for anything," said Georgij.

"Everybody wants something."

"I came here to tell you what is going to happen to you and I have told you." He stood up. "I would have been prepared to hear anything you said while I was in the room. I am going to be out of it in about a minute and after that I am not going to answer the telephone."

Mr Byun went and stood at the window.

"The fourth one," he said. "The man. You said four and you have named two of them and you have not named him, so you do not have him."

Georgij did not answer that at all.

"He is not like the others," said Mr Byun. "I want that considered. I want it taken into account that I am telling you and that I did not have to."

"Nothing is going to be taken into account. I am not trading with you and I want that said before you say another word, because if you say it thinking that you are buying something you are going to be angry with yourself tomorrow."

Mr Byun said it anyway.

"I did not take money from him."

He turned round from the window.

"Three of them came to me. The woman twice, and the old lady's man with the envelope. That is how it worked and that is what I did for twenty-six years." He had both hands on the back of a chair. "The fourth one did not come to me at all. I was told."

"By whom."

"By the owner. Personally, in his room, standing up, and it took under a minute and he did not sit down and neither did I."

Georgij stood very still.

"Say the whole of it."

"He said there was a lot going out that evening and there would be no invoice for it. I was to enter it and take it out again in the spring, and I was not to write anything at all about who had it." Mr Byun's voice had gone flat, which is what happens to men of that sort when they get to a thing they have kept. "And then he said the part I did not understand and have not understood since. He said the fee had been paid and I was to put it through the book as though it had come to me."

"He gave you the money."

"He gave me the money. In the room. From his own pocket, in an envelope that had already been made up." Mr Byun looked at the carpet. "I have taken cash from a great many people in that building and every single one of them wanted something for it. That is the only time in twenty-six years that anybody has ever given me money so that the money would be in the right place."

The flat was very quiet.

"You wrote something down," said Georgij.

"How do you know that."

"Because you were frightened, and a frightened man of that kind writes one line and puts it somewhere and then does not look at it for four years."

Mr Byun went into another room and was gone for about two minutes, and came back with a folded sheet out of a diary.

One line, a date, and four words about where the instruction came from.

He held it out and Georgij took it and read it and folded it again.

"Now the four are still going to be told," said Georgij.

"You have got what you came for."

"I did not come for that. I did not know it existed until twenty minutes ago and I did not ask you for it." He put it in the inside pocket. "You offered it to me because you thought you were buying something. I told you before you opened your mouth that you were not, and you did it anyway, and that is the whole of what has happened in this room."

Mr Byun stood in the middle of his sitting room.

"Then you are a very cruel young man."

"No," said Georgij. "I have been in three rooms in the last three weeks and in two of them that would have been true. In this one it is not, and you have not got the first idea why, and I am not going to explain it to you."

He got as far as the door.

"Twenty-six years," said Mr Byun, behind him. "Do you know what that building was like when I started in it."

"No."

"Nobody does. That is what I am going to be left with."

Georgij stopped with his hand on the door and did not turn round.

"Mr Byun."

"What."

"Mrs Sunwoo paid you to take an evening out of a book three years ago, and she has carried the word round with her ever since like a receipt." He said it perfectly evenly. "She is eighty-one. When I told her in March that it had not been done, she rang a bell and had a catalogue brought out of a drawer. She found the line with one finger and did not need to be shown which one."

"You will be able to tell her all about the building when she comes."

He had not decided any of it before he went up.

He went there with two things, which were four dates and an address. He had worked out on the stairs that there is nothing available that hurts a man of that sort except what other people think of him.

The rest of it he decided standing in a sitting room, in the four seconds after a man of sixty-eight had to try to remember which four.

That is what it is now. Nobody put a ceiling on it, nobody is going to audit it, and there is no room he has to walk into afterwards and say what it cost.

He went down eight floors and out onto a street by the river, and read the four words again in the car, and did not like where they pointed.

---

# Book Two · Chapter 23: Three of the four
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 1.3 · EN

## Day Two Hundred and Twenty-Two · Wednesday 13 May

He noticed it in the car before they were off the bridge on the sixth of May, and he has not been able to do anything about it since.

He stood in a sitting room in Seongdong and told a man of sixty-eight that the four buyers were going to be told.

There are four buyers and he can reach three.

The fourth is the man he is looking for.

That is not a technicality and he is not going to let himself have it as one. He said a thing in a room that will be three quarters true, and Mr Byun will go to his grave not knowing which quarter, and the only person who is ever going to notice is Georgij.

He wrote it in the notebook under the date, in four words, and did not soften any of them.

Mrs Sunwoo received him in the same room, at the same time of day, with the same lamp on.

She did not stand up this time and she said so.

"I stood up in March because I did not know what you were and it seemed safer. Sit down and tell me why you have come back, and do not do the thing where you say one sentence and stop, because I am eighty-one and it was charming once."

"His name is Byun," said Georgij. "He kept the register for twenty-six years and he left at the end of February. He took your money in an envelope from a man you sent, three years ago, and he did not do the thing you paid for and he did not intend to."

Mrs Sunwoo sat very still.

"Say the rest of it."

"There were four of them and you are one. He could not remember which four when I put it to him, and he was not being difficult about it. He was trying."

She did not say anything for a while.

"Where is he," she said.

"Eighth floor of a building in Seongdong with a view of the river that he paid forty per cent extra for. His daughter-in-law's family is in the same building."

"That was more than you needed to tell me."

"Yes."

She looked at him for a moment.

"You are inviting me to do something."

"I am giving you a fact that belongs to you and that I obtained without asking your permission, and I would have brought it to you if there had been nothing at the end of it at all." He did not look away. "What you do is yours. I have not got a view and I would not give you one if I had."

"That is exactly what you said in March," said Mrs Sunwoo, "and it was true in March as well, and I have not decided yet whether that makes it better."

She rang the bell and asked for the diary.

"I am going to be very slow about this," she said, when it came. "It will take about three weeks and it will not involve one raised voice or one untrue word, and at the end of it he will not be asked to anything for the rest of his life."

"Yes."

"You do not want to know how."

"No. I want you to be able to say afterwards that you did not tell me."

Mrs Sunwoo wrote three names in the diary in a hand that has not changed since 1971.

"Two things before you go," she said. "The first is that the house on the river is finished and I did not do very much of that. Eleven people did it and I only had to be the first one to say it out loud, and I have been thinking about that since the end of April."

"Say the second."

"You did not come here in March to give me anything," she said. "You came here because you needed the house shut, and telling me the truth was the only way to get it, and both of those are the case at once. I worked that out in about four minutes after you left and I have never held it against you."

"No," said Georgij. "You should."

"I am eighty-one. I have not the time to hold things against people who tell me the truth. There are so few of them that I would run out of afternoons."

* * *

He told Annie on the Thursday, in the small room, standing, in the same form he had used with Mrs Sunwoo.

"You are aware that I already know all of this," said Annie.

"Yes."

"You have known me since October and you are standing at the corner of my own desk reciting my own dates back to me."

"Yes, Mistress."

"Then say why you are doing it, because you have a reason and I would like to hear it before I decide whether it is a good one."

"Because you are two of the four," said Georgij. "October and February. If I tell Mrs Sunwoo formally and tell you across a supper table, then you are a different sort of buyer from her, and you are not. You paid the same man the same money for the same thing and he did the same nothing with it."

Annie looked at him.

"That is a better reason than I expected," she said.

"There is a second one and it is worse."

"Go on."

"I told a man in Seongdong that four people would be told. I can reach three." He said it flatly. "Doing all three properly is the only part of that sentence I have any control over, and I would rather do the part I can than decide that the whole thing was approximate."

She did not answer that immediately.

"Sit down," she said. "You have been standing in doorways for a week and it is beginning to look like a position."

He sat down on the floor at the side of the desk.

* * *

"Now the other thing," said Annie. "You have had it since Wednesday and you have not brought it to me, and that is not the arrangement, because there is not an arrangement any more and you know exactly what I mean."

"The owner," said Georgij.

"The owner."

He got the notebook out and did not open it, because he has had it by heart since the seventh.

"The house had one owner from 2011 until the winter," he said. "A man called Yeom. He is sixty-three. He did not build it and he did not particularly want it. He ran it the way a man runs a thing he inherited, which is to say he did not look at it very much and it made money."

"And in the winter."

"In the winter he sold it to the fund."

"And he took a position with them," said Annie.

Georgij looked up at that.

"You knew."

"I did not know. I have watched four men sell four things to four funds since I was twenty-six, and three of them took a position. A fund does not want the building. It wants the man who knows what is in it." She was not being clever about it. "Say where the position is."

"An address in Jung-gu," said Georgij. "Third floor. There are ten companies registered at it and none of them has any staff."

The clock in the hall did the thing it does.

"That is the address," said Annie.

"That is the address I stopped at on the night of the sixteenth of March, at twenty past one. I did not order the filings for it. I did not send for the register extract, and I left the whole of it exactly as I found it."

"And you were right to."

"I was right to. It was the correct thing and I have not changed my mind about one part of it." He put his hands flat on the floor beside him. "And the man who told Byun to put a lot through the book without an invoice is sitting on the third floor of it, and has been since January."

Annie did not say anything for a long moment.

"Say what you want to do," she said, "and do not put it as a question, because you decide these now and I am not going to let you make me do it by asking."

"I want to go at the address."

"Then say the thing you are not saying."

"The last time I stood in front of that structure I behaved correctly," said Georgij, "and behaving correctly in front of a man who reads arrangements is the same as signing my name, and you told me that in December and again in March and you were right both times." He did not soften it. "Backing away was invisible. Going at it will not be. There is no version where I do this and stay the man he cannot see."

"No."

"So the question is not whether it is dangerous. It is whether I am prepared to stop being the only thing about me that has been useful since March."

Annie got up and went to the window.

"You have had ten weeks of being the one man in Seoul he cannot see," she said, "and it has bought you a woman in Ulsan, a broker in Jung-gu, a registrar in Seongdong and a house that no longer exists. Say what it has not bought you."

"Him."

"It has not bought you him. It was never going to." She turned round. "Invisibility is a thing you spend. It is not a thing you keep, and a man who keeps it for ever has spent his whole life not being seen and has nothing at the end of it except that."

Georgij sat on the floor and did not move.

"That is not permission," he said.

"No. It is not permission and it is not an instruction, and you are going to have to decide it, and you are going to have to do it in a room with nobody in it but you." She came back to the desk. "But you asked me a fortnight ago to put the ceiling back on and I said no, and I am not going to spend the first difficult one pretending I have not got an opinion."

"You have got an opinion."

"I have got an opinion, and it is that you have been carrying a very expensive thing around for seven weeks in case it came in useful, and that it is the fourteenth of May and it has not."

He sat with that for a while.

"There is one more thing you should have before you decide," said Annie. "It is not going to help and I am going to give it to you anyway. It is the second piece I have taken off that shelf since December, and I would like you to notice that I am counting."

"I have noticed."

"The man you are looking for does not own anything at that address either," she said. "Not one share of one company on that floor. He is not on any of it and he never has been."

"Then what is he."

"He is the reason ten companies with no staff are on the third floor of a building in Jung-gu," said Annie, "and there is no document anywhere in this country that says so, and there never will be, and that is what you are proposing to walk towards."

---

# Book Two · Chapter 24: Ten minutes on a Friday
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 1.1 · EN

## Day Two Hundred and Twenty-Four · Friday 15 May

He did it at twenty past nine on the Friday morning, at the desk with the east window, and it took ten minutes.

There was nobody in the room. Nobody had put a ceiling on it, nobody was going to audit it, and there was no room he had to walk into afterwards and say what it had cost.

That is what Annie meant on the thirtieth of April and he had not understood the size of it until he was doing it.

What he ordered is the dullest thing in this country.

A commercial register extract on ten companies at one address in Jung-gu. Officers, shareholdings, changes in the last five years, and the filings that go with them. It is a form and a fee and it is done about four thousand times a day by clerks who are thinking about lunch.

It goes in with a date on it and the name of the person who asked.

He put his own name on it, because there is nobody else's he could have used.

That is the whole of the decision. It is not a raid and it is not a threat and there is no version of it that anybody could complain about, and it is the single most visible thing he has done since October.

Somebody on the third floor of that building has an arrangement with somebody at that registry, or does not need one, and either way there will be a line in a log by Tuesday saying who asked and when.

He sat with the receipt for about a minute afterwards and waited to feel something about it.

Nothing came. It is a form and a fee.

He told Annie at seven that evening and she did not ask him one question about it.

"When," she said.

"Twenty past nine this morning."

"And you have been walking round this house since twenty past nine this morning without saying anything."

"Yes."

"Good," said Annie.

He waited for the rest of it and the rest of it did not come. She went back to what she was doing, and that is the whole of what was said in that house about the largest decision he has made in his life.

That is also what she meant on the thirtieth of April, and it is worse than the first part.

The Saturday and the Sunday were the two longest days he has had since October.

There is nothing whatever to do about a form once it has gone in. He read three things he had already read. He went twice to a window that looks at a drive. At some point on the Sunday afternoon he found himself standing in the hall for a reason he could not afterwards account for.

Jang saw him do it and did not say anything about it.

Half an hour later there were two men on the gate instead of one, and there have been two on it every day since, and nobody has mentioned that either.

Nothing happened about the form on the Monday.

Something else did. On the Monday the first of the five was signed.

Mr Kwon's partner, sixty-three, sold his half at a price that was arrived at in one meeting and did not have to be argued about. Then he went out of the office and sat in his own car for a while before he started it.

He is going to live about forty minutes from his daughter. Mr Kwon telephoned Georgij about it and Georgij did not take the call, because there was nothing to say into it that was not either a lie or unkind.

The buyer of the half is a company that has existed since 2019, has an office in Busan and one employee, and has never previously bought anything.

Nobody on that coast has looked into it and nobody is going to, because a man who has just been paid a real price does not spend the afternoon on a register search.

Nothing happened on the Tuesday morning either.

At twenty to three on the Tuesday afternoon the telephone in the small room rang, which happens perhaps six times a day, and Mrs Seo answered it in the hall as she does.

She came up herself, which she does not.

"There is a gentleman on the telephone," she said. "He has asked for you by name and he has your name correctly, and he has not asked for her at all."

Georgij stood up from the desk.

"Did he give one."

"He gave a name and a company and both of them are real, because I asked him to spell the company and he spelled it without any hesitation and did not sound annoyed about it." Mrs Seo did not move from the doorway. "I have been answering that telephone for nine years and nobody has ever asked for you."

"No."

"That is what I came up about."

He went down and took it in the small room with the door open, because there was no reason to shut it and because shutting it would have been a decision as well.

The voice was about sixty and completely comfortable.

"My name is Yeom," he said. "You put a form in on Friday morning about an address in Jung-gu, and I am on the third floor of it." He was not hurrying. "I thought it would be quicker to telephone you than to let you spend four hundred thousand won finding out that there is nothing in those filings."

"That was quick," said Georgij.

"It was Friday. It is Tuesday. That is not quick, that is a long weekend." He was not being clever about it, which was the unpleasant part. "I would have telephoned on Monday and my secretary was away."

Georgij did not say anything for a moment.

"You are not going to ask me how I know it was you," said Mr Yeom.

"No."

"Why not."

"Because you have just told me. You said it in the second half of your first sentence and you did not notice, or you did notice and you wanted me to have it." Georgij kept his voice completely level. "Either way it is the same information."

There was a pause on the other end of it that was about half a second longer than it needed to be.

"That is a very good answer," said Mr Yeom. "I am going to think about it later. Now. You have been in the register at that house. You have been to see Byun in Seongdong. You were in the building at the beginning of May talking to Hwang. I do not know what you want and I would like to."

"I would like to as well," said Georgij.

"I am sorry?"

"You have just told me three things I did about a house you sold in December, and you told them to me in the order they happened, and you have been out of that business for five months." He did not soften it. "So I would like to know what you want, and I have got a stronger reason for asking than you have."

Mr Yeom laughed at that.

It was a real one and it went on for about two seconds, and there was nothing anywhere in it that he could take hold of.

"Come and have lunch," he said.

The room did the thing rooms do.

"When?" said Georgij.

"Thursday. There is a place near the office and I have had the same table there since 2016, and it is not private and I want you to know that in advance. Four other people will be able to see us the whole time."

"Who else is coming."

"Nobody. It is lunch. If I wanted a room with people in it I would have suggested a room with people in it."

Georgij stood in the small room with the telephone in his hand and the door open.

"Thursday," he said.

"Half past twelve. I will telephone you here on Wednesday to confirm it and I will ask for you by name again. I would like you to notice that I am doing that rather than having somebody bring you a card."

"I have noticed."

"Good," said Mr Yeom, and put the telephone down.

He stood there for a while afterwards with his hand still on it.

There is one more thing in that telephone call and he got to it on the stairs.

Mr Yeom knows he was in that building at the beginning of May. There was nobody at the desk that day, nobody in the corridor, and one man in a room with three crates.

Either Hwang told him, which is entirely possible and which Georgij would not hold against a man who tells everybody everything as a matter of principle.

Or somebody has opened a visitors' book that has been in a crate since the eighth of May, and read two lines in Georgij's own handwriting with the time in and the time out.

He wrote both of them himself, at a desk nobody was standing at.

It seemed to him at the time that a man who signs a book when somebody is watching should sign it when nobody is.

Then he went up and wrote four lines and looked at the fourth one for a long time.

The first three are ordinary. A man of sixty-three, out of a business he sold, tracking a register search inside two working days and being pleasant about it.

The fourth is not ordinary and it is the reason he is going.

*Lunch.*

On the twenty-seventh of April, in the small room, Annie gave him one true thing about the man he is looking for and it was not a name.

He does not eat in front of people he is working on. It is not a discipline and it is not a superstition. He simply cannot do both at once, and he has never in his life noticed that it shows.

Every man in this city who has ever sat down to a meal with him and watched him eat is a man he was not working on.

Somebody has just invited Georgij to lunch.

He does not know whether Mr Yeom is that man, or one of that man's, or somebody who has been told to do a thing and not told why. He is not going to know that on Thursday either.

He knows one thing and it is worth the four hundred thousand won he is not now going to spend.

A table at half past twelve is a table at which somebody is going to have to decide whether to eat.

He went down at about eight and told Annie all of it in order, and she listened to the whole of it without moving.

"He telephoned this house," she said, at the end.

"He telephoned this house and asked for me by name and did not ask for you, and Mrs Seo came upstairs about it, which she has not done in nine years."

Annie looked at the window for a moment.

"Then it is done," she said. "You spent it on Friday morning at twenty past nine and it took four working days to arrive." She did not look away. "I want you to hear me say that I think you were right, because I am not going to be able to say it again after Thursday."

"Why not."

"Because after Thursday one of two things will be true," said Annie. "Either you have found the bottom of it, in which case nobody is going to care what I thought on a Tuesday. Or somebody sat down opposite you and did not eat, and after that neither of us is going to have the luxury of an opinion about anything."

---

# Book Two · Chapter 25: Four people who could see us
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 1.1 · EN

## Day Two Hundred and Thirty · Thursday 21 May

He counted the room on the way in, because he counts rooms.

Nine tables. Six of them taken at twenty-five past twelve. Two exits, one of which is a kitchen and therefore not one. A window on the street side with the blind at half and the light coming in low off a white building opposite.

Mr Yeom's table is the fourth from the door on the left and it has a reserved card on it that has been used a great many times.

He is sixty-three and looks about that, and he stood up.

"You came," he said. "I told my wife you would and she said you would not, and she is right about people about seventy per cent of the time, so I am going to enjoy this for a moment."

He ordered without looking at anything and told the waiter what Georgij was having as well, and then apologised for it in the same breath and did not offer to change it.

The food came in about twelve minutes.

Mr Yeom ate it.

He ate it the way a man of sixty-three eats a thing he has eaten at the same table since 2016, which is to say without ceremony and with obvious pleasure. He talked while he did it and did not put his chopsticks down between sentences.

Georgij watched him do it and did not relax by one degree.

It rules out one man. That is all it rules out and it is worth having and it is not worth anything else.

"You are not eating," said Mr Yeom.

"I am eating."

"You are moving it about. I have watched a great many people not eat at this table and you are doing it better than most, and I would like to say for the record that I have noticed."

"I will eat it in a minute."

"Then I shall enjoy that too."

He put down what he was holding and became a different man in about a second and a half.

"Now," said Mr Yeom. "I sold that house in December to people who had been asking me for it for two years, and I took a position with them, and there is nothing in either of those sentences that anybody could make interesting."

"No."

"And then in March a woman in Ulsan got a page in the post. In April my old house shut. And in May a young man walked into a register office and put his own name on a form about the third floor of my building." He was looking straight at him. "I would like to know what you are, and I am telling you plainly that I would like to know, because I have found that asking works about as often as anything else."

"What did the fund tell you?" said Georgij.

Mr Yeom picked his chopsticks back up.

"That is not an answer."

"No. It is the question I am going to answer yours with, and you can have it in that order or not at all."

He considered that for a moment and then did the thing that told Georgij more than anything else in the hour.

He answered it.

"They told me to find out and to be pleasant about it," he said. "Those were the two things and there was not a third, and I would like you to understand that I am repeating the whole of the instruction and not the part that suits me."

"Who told you?"

"A man on the telephone whose name I know and which I am not going to give you." He said it comfortably. "Not because of any of this. Because I have worked for seven people in my life and I have never once handed one of them to anybody, and if I started at sixty-three I would not know who I was in the afternoon."

Georgij put his chopsticks down.

"That is a good reason," he said, "and it is the first one anybody has given me since March that I am not going to argue with."

"Now yours."

"I work for the woman who owns the two entries," said Georgij. "October and February. She paid your register four times over seven weeks for a deletion and got a strike-through, and the man who took the money is Byun and he could not remember which four buyers he had done it to."

Mr Yeom stopped with his chopsticks halfway.

"Four times," he said.

"Four visits. Two entries."

"He told me it was once."

That is when Georgij understood that the hour was going to be worth something after all, and it was not going to be worth what he had come for.

"Say the rest of it," he said.

"He came to me in February of last year," said Mr Yeom. "He was frightened. He said a buyer had been very persistent about a deletion and that he had taken money for it and that he wanted me to know before somebody else told me." He put the chopsticks down properly this time. "He said it was one. He said it had happened once in twenty-six years and would not happen again. I gave him a great deal of trouble about it for about ten minutes and then I let it go, because he had come to me."

"He had come to you."

"That is the whole of why I let it go. A man who tells you a thing you would never have found out has not got anything else in his pocket." Mr Yeom looked at the table. "That was my view for a very long time and I have just watched it stop being one."

"There were four," said Georgij. "October, February, one three years ago and one four years ago."

"Say the fourth one again."

"Four years ago."

Mr Yeom did not say anything at all for about six seconds.

"I paid that one," he said.

The room did what rooms do, and at the third table from the window a man of about sixty who is not tall put a spoon down beside a bowl he has not touched.

Georgij saw it in the edge of his vision and did not turn his head one degree.

"Say that properly," he said.

"Somebody asked me for a favour four years ago," said Mr Yeom. "A lot going out with no invoice, entered and taken out again in the spring, nothing written down about who had it. And I gave Byun the fee out of my own pocket in an envelope. The book showed a payment in the ordinary way and nobody in that building ever wondered why there had not been one."

"And you did that for whom."

"For a man who telephoned me and said he was asking on behalf of somebody who would be grateful."

"You did not ask who."

"No," said Mr Yeom, and he said it without any embarrassment at all, "and I would like you to understand exactly why not, because you are going to think it was cowardice and it was not. It was arithmetic."

"Say the arithmetic."

"A man who telephones you and does not say a name is offering you a thing worth more than a name." He had both hands flat on either side of the plate. "If I ask who, then he tells me, and then I have done a favour for a person and I am owed by a person. If I do not ask, then I am owed by whoever it is, and whoever it is knows that I did not ask, and that is worth about ten times as much and it costs nothing."

"And it paid."

"It paid in December," said Mr Yeom. "Somebody had been asking me for that house for two years. In the winter the price came up by rather more than the house was worth, and there was a position in it for me." He did not look away. "I did not have to ask about that either."

Georgij sat at the fourth table from the door on the left.

"So you have never known," he said.

"I have never known and I have never wanted to, and I have spent four years being extremely comfortable about that." Mr Yeom looked at him. "And you have just sat down opposite me and told me that a woman in Ulsan is selling trucks, and I find that I am doing arithmetic again and I do not like the answer."

"Do not say the rest of that out loud in this room."

Mr Yeom's face changed by about a millimetre.

"Why not."

"Because you have just told me you do not know who you did it for," said Georgij, "and that is the only thing keeping you comfortable, and it is a great deal safer than knowing."

Neither of them said anything for a moment.

"There is one more thing and then I am going to eat this," said Georgij, "because I said I would and because I would like to have been the sort of person who did."

"Go on."

"The man who telephoned you four years ago. Not the one behind him. The one who made the call." He kept his voice completely level. "Did he ever say anything that was not necessary."

Mr Yeom thought about that properly, which took about twenty seconds, and Georgij let it.

"He said he was glad we had met," said Mr Yeom.

"And had you."

"No. That is why I remember it. I put the telephone down and I said to my wife that a man had just told me it was nice to see me again and I had never laid eyes on him."

Georgij ate what was in front of him.

It took about four minutes and it was very good and he could not afterwards have said one thing about it.

At the door Mr Yeom shook his hand and did not make anything of it.

"You are going to tell me that I should be worried," he said.

"No."

"Everybody tells me that."

"You have been worried since the words *four years ago* and you have been eating anyway, and I have watched a great many people do the other thing." Georgij did not soften it. "You do not need me for this part."

He went out past six tables.

At the third one from the window there is a man of about sixty who is not tall, sitting alone, in the sort of suit that costs a great deal and is designed so that nobody can tell.

There is a bowl in front of him and a spoon beside it. The bowl has not been touched. It has been there for the whole of an hour and ten minutes and it has gone cold.

He was reading something on the table and he did not look up.

Georgij did not look at him.

He did not slow down, he did not turn his head, and he did not do the thing a man does with his shoulders when he has decided not to look at something.

He has been practising that since October and it has never once mattered until today.

Out on the street he stood for a moment in the low light off a white building and did the arithmetic he had been holding since the third table.

There are three possibilities and he can put them in order.

The man is nobody, and a man of sixty with no appetite ordered a bowl and read something.

The man is somebody and did not know Georgij would be there, in which case Georgij has just seen him and he does not know it.

Or the man is somebody, and knew exactly when the table was booked, and came to look at the thing that has been walking round his arrangement since March.

He does not know which and he is not going to.

What he knows is that Mr Yeom ate everything in front of him and talked while he did it. And that a man three tables away sat for an hour and ten minutes with a bowl going cold.

Both of those are facts. Only one of them is a fact about Georgij, and he does not know which.

---

# Book Two · Chapter 26: The third name on a list of three
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 1.1 · EN

## Day Two Hundred and Thirty-Six · Wednesday 27 May

The reason he did not do this in March is that doing it would have told Park Sang-hoon a great deal.

That reason went into a register office at twenty past nine on the fifteenth of May with his own name on it, and there is nothing left of it, and he has known that since the Tuesday.

It is a launch, which is four hours in a shed with a hundred and forty people in it and a hull that everybody has already seen photographs of.

Anybody may come. That is the whole of the point and it is why Sang-hoon said *four or five of these a year* in a car park in March and not *four or five dinners*.

Georgij stood in the second room, near the doors, with his hands where they could be seen, and did not speak to anybody.

It took nineteen minutes.

Sang-hoon came over himself, through about forty people, and two of them tried to intercept him on the way and did not manage it.

"You came and stood in a corridor," he said.

"There is not a corridor. This is the second room."

"There is never a corridor. It is a figure of speech and you knew what I meant in March and you have known it for two months." He was not smiling and he was not not smiling. "Ask."

"I want to describe a man to you," said Georgij. "I am not going to say a name, because I have not got one, and I am not going to tell you why until you have answered."

"Then describe him."

He gave the nine lines.

About sixty or a little less. Not tall. Pleasant, quiet, the sort of man who has been in rooms. Sits on nothing, is in no report, gives dinners. Is introduced in a way that does not carry a name. Arranges two people into a room and goes home.

He did not give the ninth one yet.

Sang-hoon looked at him for about two seconds.

"I know who that is," he said. "Say the last one anyway, because you are holding one and I would like to hear it."

"He tells people he is glad they have finally met," said Georgij. "The first time. When they have never met."

Sang-hoon did not move at all.

"Yes," he said.

Somebody made a speech in the first room and about sixty people applauded it and neither of them turned round.

"Now the question," said Georgij. "It is one question and it is going to sound absurd."

"Ask it."

"Have you ever watched him eat?"

Sang-hoon stood in the second room of a shed with a hull in it and did not answer for what was probably four seconds.

"Say why you want to know," he said, "because I have got the answer and I am not going to give it to you until I know what it is worth, and I have never said that to you before."

"He does not eat in front of people he is working on," said Georgij. "It is not a discipline and it is not a superstition. He cannot do both at once and he has never in his life noticed that it shows."

"Who told you that."

"Somebody who has known him for a very long time and who will not give me his name."

"That is the most expensive sentence anybody has said to me this year," said Sang-hoon, "and you have just put it in front of me for nothing, and I would like a moment."

He took it. He stood there and took about eleven seconds of it in a room with a hundred and forty people in it, and nobody came near him, because men of that sort are not approached when they are standing still.

"Eight years," he said. "Twenty-something occasions. Dinners at his own house, dinners at other people's, two lunches, a thing on a boat in 2019."

"And."

"And I have never once seen him put anything in his mouth," said Park Sang-hoon.

The shed was very loud and it made no difference whatever.

"Not once," he said. "Not one thing. He has a glass in his hand and he moves it about, and he is the best company in the room, and everybody goes home pleased. I have known that man for eight years and I could not tell you one thing that he likes."

Georgij did not say anything.

There was nothing available that was both true and survivable, and this is the fifth time in his life he has been in that position and the second one in six weeks.

"Say the rest of it," said Sang-hoon. "You are working out how to put it and there is no way of putting it. Say it flat."

"He has been working on you for eight years," said Georgij.

"Yes."

"You did not know."

"I did not know. I have been sitting at that man's table since I was fifty-one." He said it very quietly and it did not sound like anything at all. "I have made four decisions at those dinners that I have never been able to account for afterwards, and I put every one of them down to being tired."

Nobody said anything for a while.

"There is a second thing," said Georgij, "and it is worse, and I am going to give it to you because I am not going to be a person who tells you the first half."

"Go on."

"In November I did the same thing to you," he said. "Nineteen pages and a mistake on page nine. I told you exactly how, on the eleventh of March, in a corridor at a funeral." He did not soften it. "You took it, and you said the worst of it was that you would do the three and a half hours again." He did not look away. "If it turns out that a man had already been doing it to you for six years before I got there, then I did not aim you. I found the handle where somebody else had left it."

Sang-hoon looked at him.

"That is a filthy thing to have to hear," he said, "and it is the second one you have brought me, and you have not asked me for anything yet."

"No."

"Why not."

"Because I came here to ask you one question and you have answered it," said Georgij, "and the answer was no, and no is what I came for. Everything since then has been mine to give you and not the other way round."

Park Sang-hoon put his glass down on a table that was not intended for glasses.

"Choi Dae-ho," he said.

Georgij stood absolutely still.

"That is what you came for," said Sang-hoon. "You did not ask and you were not going to ask, and I have watched you not ask for four minutes. I would like it on the record that I gave it to you and that you did not."

"Say it again."

"Choi Dae-ho. Fifty-nine. Former prosecutor, out early and nobody says why. He is a director of a private equity firm in Hong Kong through a chain of lawyers in Singapore and he has never made an investment in his life." He said it flatly and completely. "He is not a financier. He does not own anything. He gives dinners."

The twenty-fifth of October.

A ballroom, twelve minutes in, a man of sixty-one at the same table for nineteen years asked whether he had been trained abroad, and then gave him three names to keep away from, in order.

Woo. Sunwoo. Choi Dae-ho.

Third.

He has had it since the fourteenth minute of the first evening.

"You have gone somewhere," said Sang-hoon.

"I was given that name on the twenty-fifth of October by a man called Hong, at a table, in a list of three." Georgij's voice did not change at all. "He put it third. He said it as a kindness and I wrote all three down and I have looked at that page perhaps twenty times since."

"And you did not know."

"I did not know. It has been in my notebook for seven months in the third position, which is where a man puts the one he actually means."

"That is not a failure," said Sang-hoon.

"It is the largest one I have."

"No," he said. "Listen to me, because I am about to be useful and I am not going to be again this year. You did not miss it. It was put in a list of three so that you would keep it and not use it. Hong is a decent man and he has never in his life understood why he gives people three names instead of one. I could not tell you either, and I have been at that table."

Georgij looked at him.

"You are saying Hong was arranged as well."

"I am saying that I have spent eight years having dinner with a man who has never eaten." He was not looking at Georgij. "As of about six minutes ago I do not know how far out that goes, and neither do you, and I am not going to pretend it is a comfortable position for either of us."

Somebody in the first room said something about the hull and about a hundred people applauded again.

"What are you going to do," said Sang-hoon.

"I do not know yet."

"That is the first time you have said that to me."

"It is the first time it has been true in front of you."

Park Sang-hoon picked his glass back up and did not drink out of it.

"When you do know," he said, "you are going to want something, and you are not going to come and ask me for it, and we have had that conversation and I am not having it again." He looked out at the shed. "So I am going to say one thing instead and then I am going to go and be pleasant to a hundred and forty people."

"Say it."

"I have four decisions I cannot account for," said Sang-hoon. "You will find out what all four of them were before I do, because that is what you are for and we both know it. When you find them, I want them. Not the man. The four."

"Why not the man."

"Because the man is yours," he said, "and I have watched you for seven months and I am not going to be in the way of that. And because I have had a great deal taken off me this year by people who were entitled to, and I would like the four things back that I gave away for nothing."

He went.

Georgij stood by the doors of the second room for another twenty minutes because leaving straight away would have been the signature, and then went out to the car.

He wrote one line in the notebook before Mr Ku started it.

Not the name. He does not need to write down the name.

*Hong. 25 Oct. Third of three. Ask who put him at that table.*

There is a thing he has to do this evening and he worked out on the bridge that there is no good way of doing it.

He is going to walk into the small room and say a name out loud that she has been holding since December.

She is going to have to stand there and hear him say it, from a man who wanted to buy him, at a launch, for nothing.

Neither of them chose the afternoon.

---

# Book Two · Chapter 27: What he collects
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 1.2 · EN

## Day Two Hundred and Thirty-Six · Wednesday 27 May

He had four hours between the shed and the drive and he spent about three of them deciding how to say it.

Every version he built was a way of making it easier for her, and he took all of them apart on the bridge. A man who arranges the sentence has decided in advance what the other person is going to feel about it.

He came in at ten past eight and did not sit down and did not put it off.

"Choi Dae-ho," he said.

Two words, and nothing at either end of them, and then he stood there and let her have it in whatever shape it came.

Annie was at the desk with a pen in her hand and she did not put it down.

She did not move at all for about two seconds. Then she finished the word she was in the middle of. Then she put the pen down.

The order of those three things is the whole of what happened on her face.

"Who," she said.

"Park Sang-hoon. This afternoon, at a launch, in the second room. He gave it to me without being asked and he said so out loud while he was doing it."

"For nothing."

"For nothing."

"Then he is a better man than I have ever been prepared to say," said Annie, "and I am going to have to sit with that, and not tonight."

She got up and went to the window.

"How did he come to give it to you."

"I asked him one question and it was the one you gave me on the twenty-seventh of April." Georgij did not move from the corner of the desk. "Whether he had ever watched him eat."

"And."

"Eight years. Twenty-something occasions. Not one thing, not once, and he had never noticed until I put it in front of him."

Annie stood at the window with the curtain not drawn and the drive dark behind the glass.

"He will be at his own table tonight," she said, "and he will be eating, and he will be extremely good company, and there will be four people there who think they are having dinner."

"You have said that as though you have seen it."

"I have seen it fifteen times."

Georgij did not say anything.

"Sit down," said Annie. "Not on the floor. In the chair, where I can see the whole of you, because I am going to give you the rest of it now and I would like to watch you take it."

He sat in the chair.

"I have known him since I was twenty-four," she said, and then stopped.

"That is not true and I have said it for so long that it comes out by itself." She did not apologise for it and did not make anything of it. "I was fourteen. He was at my father's table before I was allowed at it, and he was doing then what he does now, and I was the only person in that house who ever noticed that he did not eat."

"Fourteen."

"I noticed at fourteen and I did not understand it until I was about thirty. By then it was a thing I had always known and I could not remember not knowing it."

The clock in the hall did the thing it does.

"In December I gave you a reason for holding his name and it was a true one," said Annie. "In March I gave you a second and it was also true. On the twenty-seventh of April I gave you the best one I had, which is that the name makes you legible, and every word of that was true as well."

"And."

"And none of them is why."

She came away from the window.

"Say the real one," said Georgij.

"He collects."

Nobody said anything at all for a moment.

"Say it properly," said Georgij.

"You know what I mean and you knew inside a second, and I would rather say it once and completely than have you sit there assembling it." She stood in the middle of the room. "He buys people. At that house and at two others. He has been doing it for a very long time and I do not know how many and I have never wanted to."

"The fourth line."

There are four things that have ever made Georgij put a hand on somebody, and he can name all four, and the largest of them is what grown people intend to do with children.

He did not move in that chair. His hands stayed where they were on his knees and his face did the thing it does, which is nothing at all.

Annie watched him not move and did not mistake it for one second.

"The fourth line is one of them. Four years ago, no invoice, out of the building without a bill and out of the book in the spring." She put all of it down in front of him. "And now you know why a man who could have had it for nothing paid a fee. An absence in a ledger is a question. A payment in a ledger is a Tuesday."

Georgij sat in the chair and did not move.

"How long have you known that," he said.

"About him, since I was thirty. About the fourth line, since the twentieth of March." She looked straight at him. "You stood in that room and named three people who were going to be hurt without having done anything. The third one was whoever is on the fourth line, who nobody has ever billed."

"I did the arithmetic while you were still talking," said Annie, "and then I asked you about the woman in Ulsan."

"Sixty-eight days."

"Sixty-eight days," said Annie.

He did not ask the next thing, because the next thing was in the room and asking it would have been for him and not for her.

She said it anyway.

"That is why I held the name," she said. "Not the legibility. That was a real reason and I used it because it was better than the one underneath."

"Say the one underneath."

"There is a person somewhere who was bought at that house four years ago by a man who does not eat," said Annie. "That person has no line, no owner and no date anywhere in the world, because it was taken out of the book in the spring and nobody was ever billed for it."

Georgij did not move at all.

"And?"

"And it is my sentence," she said. "I said it to you in this house on the second of January, and I said it again on the eleventh of March, and both times I was telling you the thing that keeps you alive."

The room was very quiet.

"I did not want you to find out that it is also a description of somebody who is not," said Annie. "I have known since the twentieth of March that you were going to, and I have spent sixty-eight days arranging for it to be later." Her hands stayed at her sides. "This afternoon Park Sang-hoon gave it away at a launch for nothing, and there was nothing I could do about that at all."

Georgij sat in a chair with both hands on his knees.

"That was not your decision to make," he said.

"No."

"You made it anyway."

"I made it anyway, for sixty-eight days, and I am not going to tell you that I am sorry about it, because I am not, and you would know inside a syllable." She stood where she was. "I am telling you what I did. You may do whatever you like with it."

He stood up.

"May I say two things?" he said.

"Say them."

"The first is that you were wrong and it does not matter now."

"Yes."

"The second is that I have known you for seven months and this is the first time you have ever done anything for a reason that was not exactly what you said it was." He was not accusing her of anything and it did not come out like an accusation. "And you did it about this. Out of everything in this year, you did it about this."

Annie stood in the middle of her own room.

"Yes," she said.

He did not say the rest of it. There was nothing available that was both true and small enough to survive being said, and she was standing four feet away and had just told him the only untidy thing she has ever done.

"What are you going to do," she said.

"Find the person."

"That is not what I asked."

"It is what I am going to do." He said it perfectly evenly. "The rest of it is going to happen to him and it is going to be complete and I am not going to enjoy telling you about it afterwards. But that is not what I am going to do first and it is not what any of this is for."

"He is four years gone," said Annie. "Out of a book, off a page, with no bill and no name, and I have spent sixty-eight days deciding that there is nothing at the end of that road."

"There is a person at the end of it," said Georgij, "and I know exactly what they are, and there is nobody else in this country who does."

She looked at him for a long moment.

"No," she said. "There is not."

He went as far as the door and she said one more thing, and she said it to the window and not to him, which is how she says the ones that cost her.

"You are going to be gone a great deal now."

"Yes."

"Then come and sit on the floor for an hour before you start."

He came back and sat down at the side of the desk with his back against it. She did not read anything and she did not pick the pen up again, and neither of them said one word for the whole hour.

Somewhere in the middle of it he got the first piece of it and did not say so.

A lot that was taken out of a book in the spring was in the book before the spring. It was entered. It sat on a page in that building for four or five months, with a reference and a season and a line, and it was ordinary while it was there.

Three people have looked at that page in the last four years.

Byun wrote it and took it out and has spent four years being comfortable. Hwang found the fee in December and never saw the entry, because it had gone before he was born into that building.

And a woman behind a glass panel on the first floor, at a counter with one chair on the near side, has been reading every line that house wrote for twenty-three years.

She went out of that building for the last time in the middle of May.

It is the twenty-seventh.

He does not know where she lives and he has never asked, and there is exactly one person in this country who would have written it down.

Mr Hwang keeps everything.

At some point in the middle of it she put her hand down into his hair and left it there, and he put his head back against the side of the desk.

That is the whole of what happened in that room on the twenty-seventh of May.

---

# Book Two · Chapter 28: He keeps everything
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 1.2 · EN

## Day Two Hundred and Thirty-Seven · Thursday 28 May

There is no book at the door any more.

The counter is there and the pen is gone and the shelf under it has been emptied, and a man who wanted to write his name in that building today would have nowhere to put it.

Georgij stood in front of it for a moment anyway.

Three times in eight weeks he wrote his name in a book at a door, and every one of those three has come back to him with interest.

This is the first door he has walked through this year that did not want it.

He found he did not like that either.

The building is nearly done. The first floor has no chairs in it. The glass panel at the settlement desk has been taken out of its frame and stood against the wall behind it. The slot for envelopes is a rectangular hole in a piece of plywood. The single chair on the visitor's side is gone.

He looked at the hole for about four seconds.

Mr Hwang was on the third floor in a room with two crates and a table and nothing else.

He had his coat on because the heating in that building was turned off on the first, and he was going through a box file page by page, and he did not stop when Georgij came in.

"You," said Mr Hwang.

"Yes."

Mr Hwang put the page he was holding face down on the table, squared it, and turned round.

"You have got what you came for," he said. "I remember saying it and I remember meaning it, and I have thought about that afternoon more than I am going to tell you."

"I know."

"Then say what this is."

"I have come to ask you for something," said Georgij, "and I have nothing to give you for it, and I am not going to dress it up. There is no arrangement here and there is nothing at the end of it for you."

Mr Hwang stood with one hand on the box file.

"That is at least consistent," he said.

"I want an address," said Georgij. "The woman who was behind the glass downstairs."

Mr Hwang did not go to a box and did not open anything.

"Jeon Mi-ja," he said. "Twenty-three years at that desk. Her last day was the twelfth of May and she came up here at half past four to give me the keys to the drawer, and she would not put them on the table. She put them in my hand."

He had it by heart.

"You did not have to look that up."

"No," said Mr Hwang. "I have not had to look that up once since the twelfth of May, and I have tried."

Georgij stayed near the door.

"Why did you do it?"

"Because it was correct." Mr Hwang said it without any defence in it at all, which is new. "The house is winding up. There will be no settlements. A settlement desk in a house with no settlements is a salary against nothing, and I put it in the schedule in the second week of April with eleven other posts, and every one of the twelve was correct."

"And?"

"And I did the twelve at once, in an afternoon, out of a spreadsheet." He looked at the table rather than at Georgij. "I did not go down to that floor. I have been in this building since the autumn and I have been down to that floor twice."

Georgij did not say anything at all.

"You are going to tell me that is the same thing again," said Mr Hwang.

"No," said Georgij. "You have just told me. Leave it where it is."

Mr Hwang turned back to the table and moved the box file about an inch to the left, which did not need doing.

"There is a thing you should know before I give you the address," he said, "and I am going to give it to you, and I would like to have said this first so that it is not payment."

"Say it."

"She came up here on the ninth of January." Mr Hwang put both hands flat on the table. "She sat where you are standing, because there was a chair there then, and she had a school exercise book with her that she had bought herself. She said she had been keeping a list."

The room was very cold.

"Of what," said Georgij.

"Of everything that went through that desk in twenty-three years that did not clear." He got it out in one piece and it cost him something. "Not fraud. She was careful about that word and I have thought about how careful she was. Things that went out of the room with no paper behind them. She said there were not very many and that she knew all of them."

"How many?"

"She did not say and I did not ask her."

Georgij stood in a room with two crates in it.

"What did you say to her?"

"I said that the house was in wind-up," said Mr Hwang, "and that reopening a matter in wind-up requires the administrator's consent. And that as the administrator I was not going to consent to twenty-three years of it in January, because it would not be finished before the house was gone, and an unfinished inquiry is worse than none."

He stopped.

"Every word of that is true," he said, "and I have taken it apart every night for three weeks and it is still true, and it is still the worst thing I have ever said to anybody."

"You did not read it."

"I did not open it. She had it in her hands and she was holding it out and I did not put my hand on it." Mr Hwang's voice did not change. "She put it back in her bag and she said thank you, and she went downstairs and did four more months."

Georgij looked at the two crates and did not look at Mr Hwang at all.

"Do you have it?"

"No. She took it with her and it was hers. I have the note of the meeting, because I make a note of every meeting, and it says the date and the time and one line about the subject and nothing else."

"You keep everything."

Mr Hwang looked up at that.

"She told you that," he said.

"Yes. On the eighteenth of March, through the glass, at the end of a conversation I paid her for." Georgij gave it to him whole. "It was the last thing she said to me, and she said it about you, and she said you were proud of it."

Mr Hwang stood very still for a while.

"I am going to give you the address and then I want you to leave," he said.

He went to the second crate and did not have to look for it. It was a personnel file with four things in it. He took out the second thing and copied an address onto the back of a compliments slip, printed with the name of a house that will not exist in six weeks.

He held it out. Georgij took it.

"There is a bus every twenty minutes from the station and it is the fourth stop," said Mr Hwang. "I know that because she wrote it on her application when she came and nobody has ever asked her for a new one."

Georgij put it in his inside pocket without reading it.

"She asked me for a reference on the twelfth," said Mr Hwang. "I wrote her one. It is two pages and every sentence in it is true and I have never written a better one about anybody."

He put the file back in the crate.

"And there is not a firm in this country that will read past the letterhead," he said. "I knew that while I was writing it. I wrote it anyway because it was the only thing available, and I have been unable to decide since whether that was decent or whether it was the same thing I have been doing all year."

"It was the same thing," said Georgij.

Mr Hwang nodded once, slowly, as though somebody had confirmed a figure.

"Are you going to do something for her."

"I did something in March," said Georgij. "There is a man in this city who owed me an introduction and it would cost him nothing to make it. I made him make it. A firm rang her at the end of April."

"And."

"And I do not know," said Georgij.

He could have stopped there and the room would have let him.

"I made the call and I put a line through it," he said. "I have not asked her and I have not asked him, and in ten weeks it has not once been the most urgent thing in front of me. I am here today because I want something out of her."

Mr Hwang did not say anything for a moment.

"Then we are the same sort of man after all," he said.

Georgij had an answer ready and heard what was wrong with it before he got to it.

"You did twelve in an afternoon and did not go down to the floor," he said. "I did one in a morning and did not go down either. That is a difference in size and it is not a difference in kind, and I am not going to stand in your room and pretend it is while I am asking you for something."

The heating stayed off and the room stayed cold.

"There is one thing," said Mr Hwang.

"Say it."

"If it turns out that she was right in January," he said, "I would like that put somewhere. Not for me. I am finished and I have understood that properly now, and I am not asking you to unfinish it."

He straightened the box file again.

"She sat in this room with a school exercise book that she bought with her own money, and she held it out to a man who was proud of keeping everything." His hand stayed on the file. "I would like there to be a piece of paper somewhere in the world that says she did."

Georgij stood by the door with the compliments slip in his pocket.

He did not agree to it. He also did not refuse, and Mr Hwang had been in enough rooms by now to know that he was not being answered, and he did not ask a second time.

"Mr Hwang."

"Yes."

"You will be in work by October," said Georgij. "It will be beneath you and you will do it properly, and the man who takes you on is going to get somebody who has learned the only thing you did not know in the autumn."

Mr Hwang picked the page back up off the table and turned it the right way round.

"You said that to me in May," he said. "You said eventually, and you said it would be beneath me, and I asked you whether it was supposed to be a kindness. I have had three weeks with it and I am used to it."

He put the page down square with the edge.

"Do not say it twice. The second one is not for me."

Georgij stood in a cold room and took it.

"No," he said. "It is not."

"Then there is one more thing I want and it is not the address," said Georgij. "The settlement ledgers."

Mr Hwang looked at the crate he had closed.

"Gone on the twentieth, in a van, against an inventory I signed," he said. "They are in a storage unit belonging to a fund that has no interest in what is in them. In some number of years somebody will work out what that unit costs a month, and they will be pulped."

"And you cannot get at them."

"I am not the custodian of anything in this building any more." Mr Hwang put one hand flat on the crate lid. "I could have had the settlement books copied in the first week of May and not one person would have asked me a question about it. It did not occur to me, because in the first week of May I still thought the worst thing in this house was a practice I had stopped."

"I should have asked you for them in May."

"Yes," said Mr Hwang. "You should."

He went down three floors and past the hole in the plywood and out through a door with no book at it.

Mr Ku had the car by the wall.

Georgij read the address in the car. It is four stops out of a station he has never used, in a district that does not appear once in anything he has read this year.

On the sixteenth of March she told him to come back when he had his name. She said she would tell him the rest of what she knew, and that it was not very much and was worth having.

Mr Hwang would not take the exercise book in January, and it went back out of that building in her bag the same afternoon.

The ledgers are in a unit in a yard behind a fund that has never opened them. Every other record of what did not clear at that desk is in a flat four stops out of a station he has never used. It is a school exercise book, and the woman who bought it has been given no reason at all to show it to anybody.

In March she named her price and it was a name.

He is going to arrive at her door without one.

Then he got the notebook out, under the date, and wrote two lines that he has not promised to anybody.

*Jeon Mi-ja. 9 Jan. Held it out. He did not take it.*

*She has a list.*

---

# Book Two · Chapter 29: The rest of what I know
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 1.4 · EN

## Day Two Hundred and Thirty-Eight · Friday 29 May

The fourth stop is a road with a chemist on the corner and eleven apartment blocks behind it, and the blocks are numbered in a way that made sense to somebody in about nineteen eighty.

Georgij came out on the bus.

He could have come in the car. Mr Ku offered twice and did not press it the second time, which is Mr Ku saying that he thinks it is a mistake.

A car of that kind at the door of a woman who has been out of work for seventeen days is a sentence about her, delivered to everybody on that stair. She would have had to live in it after he had gone.

Second floor. The bell has a strip of tape over the name.

She opened the door a hand's width with her hand still on it and looked at him.

"You," said Mrs Jeon.

"Yes."

She did not move.

"You do not have your name," she said. "I can see that from here."

"No."

"Then you have come to ask me for something on credit, and I told you in March what I do about that."

Georgij stood in a corridor with a strip light in it.

"May I have four minutes?" he said. "If you want me gone at the end of them I will go, and I will not come back, and nobody else will come here either."

She looked at him for what was probably three seconds. Then she took her hand off the door.

The flat is one room and a kitchen and a window with the eleven blocks in it. There is a table with two chairs, and on the table there is a school exercise book with a green cover and a biro lying across it, closed.

She had been sitting at it when he rang.

She did not put it away and she did not move it. She sat down in front of it with both hands flat on the cover, which is how a person sits who has decided that a thing is not going to be taken off the table.

"Four minutes," said Mrs Jeon.

"I have been in the house on the river since March," said Georgij. "I know what happened to your desk and I know who signed the schedule. I went and saw him yesterday afternoon and he told me about the ninth of January."

Her hands did not come off the book.

"He told you."

"He told me before he gave me your address, and he said out loud that he was telling me first so that it would not be payment." Georgij did not sit down, because he had not been asked to. "He said you had it in your hands and you were holding it out and he did not put his hand on it. He said every word of what he told you that day was true, and that it is still true, and that it is still the worst thing he has ever said to anybody."

Mrs Jeon looked at the window for a moment.

"He is not wrong that it was true," she said. "He could have opened it in January. He has had from January until May to be sorry about it, and being sorry about it is not the same as opening it, and I am not going to be the one who tells him it is."

"No."

"Sit down."

He sat down.

"Your four minutes have about ninety seconds in them," she said. "Say the thing you came to say."

"You were right," said Georgij.

Mrs Jeon did not answer that.

"Not you were probably right, and not there is something in it," he said. "The fourth reference on that back page is a person, and there was no bill because there was never going to be one. You went through it twice in twenty-three years because you were the only one in that building who could see the shape of it."

The biro rolled about an inch and stopped against her hand.

"You cannot know that," she said.

"I can."

"How."

"Because a fee was paid for that line by the wrong man," said Georgij, "on the night, out of his own pocket, in an envelope that had already been made up. Nobody billed him and nobody was ever going to. He put a payment where an absence would have been."

She did not say anything at all.

"An empty space in a ledger is a question," he said. "A payment in a ledger is an ordinary Tuesday. He was not buying a deletion. He was buying the look of one, and he paid a house he did not owe in order to have it. The only reason anybody ever noticed the space was that a woman behind a glass panel could not let it alone."

Mrs Jeon put the biro down flat.

"I sat at that desk until midnight," she said.

Georgij waited.
