He went at it the way he goes at anything, which is backward from the thing that happened. There were only ever going to be five candidates, because five kitchens in this country know that a man has been asking.

Four of them know a description and a question.

One of them knows a description, a question, and that the man who asked was telling her the truth about why he had come. He stood in front of her desk and told her, in a sentence he had chosen on the walk over.

Mrs Bae has kept a book in pencil for twenty-two years and has never in her life forgotten a booking.

A booking has a telephone number on it, because a room held for a Tuesday is a room somebody has to be able to ring about.

Georgij sat with that for a moment and then wrote it down without softening a single part of it.

*She gave me nine years of a customer because I described him before I asked and because everything I said was true. She has thought about that every night since the twenty-third of June, and on one of those nights she picked up the telephone, and she was entitled to.*

*I did not make her a promise, and she did not make me one. I walked out of there having taken something off a woman who keeps a book in pencil, and I did not once ask myself what she was going to do with the evening afterward.*

He did not write the other version. That he had done it correctly, that honesty was the reason she had answered at all, and that no route to Sim existed which did not run through somebody who would afterward have to live with it.

He did not write it because it is true, and because writing down the true thing that lets you off is how a man starts keeping a different sort of book.

* * *

Annie came in at half past ten and did not comment on the flowers for a full minute, which is how he knew she had seen them from the corridor.

"Say what they are," she said.

"Twelve white stems, no wrap. It is the order from Hyoja-dong, both slips, four years ago." Georgij had not moved from the desk. "There is no card, and the missing card is the entire message, because a card is the only part of it anybody keeps. He said that himself, four years ago, through a man who wrote it down."

Annie went and stood over them and did not touch them either.

"They came to the gate of my house," she said.

"They did."

"Then say the rest of it, because you have had an hour and a half and you have not been sitting there admiring them."

"Mrs Bae rang him." He said it flatly and completely. "The woman in the fifth kitchen. She has his booking number because he books. She gave me nine years of a customer on the twenty-third of June because I told her the truth about why I was there, and she has had three and a half weeks to be sorry about it."

"And you did not see it coming."

"I saw that it might come. I did not put a number on it and I did not tell you, and those are two different failures and I would like both of them on the table."

Annie looked at him for a while.

"Say what he knows."

"He knows a man has been asking in kitchens. He knows the man went to Seodaemun on Monday and stood in an entrance hall, because I told a woman with a watering can to tell him everything." Georgij kept his hands where they were. "And he knows this house, and I am not going to pretend that took him any cleverness at all. A man who has spent nine years being liked in private rooms asks four people who a well-dressed man with a foreign face works for, and the fourth one tells him without knowing he has told him."

"Then he has known since about Wednesday."

"Since about Wednesday."

Annie sat down on the edge of the desk, on the side away from the flowers.

"What has he not done," she said.

Georgij looked up at her then, because it was the right question and because it was not the one he had been braced for.

"He has not told Choi Dae-ho," he said slowly.

"Say why you think that."

"Because if he had, this would not be flowers." He worked it out as he said it, which he does not usually allow himself in front of her. "If that man knew, the correct move is nothing at all. He goes quiet, everything he touches goes quiet, and I stand in a corridor for the rest of the year finding out that every road I have has gone cold on the same Tuesday. That is what he does. He has done it once already this year, in April, in a kitchen."

"And instead you have flowers."

"Instead I have flowers, which is a man doing something on his own account, in his own style, without asking anybody." Georgij put one finger on the desk beside the stems. "He is not telling his employer that he was seen. A man whose entire value is that nobody can describe him does not go upstairs and report that three people can describe him."

Annie let that sit.

"That is the first thing anybody on that side has done for their own reasons and not his," she said.

"Yes."

"Then it is also the first crack." She said it without any pleasure in it at all. "Not a weakness. A seam. Those are different and you will want to remember which one this is in about a fortnight."

* * *

They did not throw the flowers away.

Annie had them put in water in the small room, on the corner of the desk, where Georgij works, and she said why in one sentence and did not elaborate on it.

"If I put them in the hall I am pretending they are flowers, and if I put them in the bin I am telling him they arrived."

Georgij wrote the day up before lunch, and it took him three attempts, because the first two were about Sim and the third one was not.

*Page two, and it is the worst line on it: this house, since Wednesday, and there is nobody I can put next to it who made it happen except me.*

Then, underneath:

*Mrs Bae, and she is not on page two, she is on page one, because she is not somebody who found out. She is somebody who acted. I have been keeping a list of people who know and I have not been keeping one of people who decide, and those are not the same list and I have needed the second one since June.*

He started it at the bottom of the page, with two names on it, and the second one was his own.

*People who did something because of what I told them.*

*Mrs Bae, 23 June. Telephoned a man.*

*A woman with a watering can, 13 July. Will tell him everything, and I asked her to.*

He looked at the second entry for a long time.

He had put her there on purpose. It had been the right thing and he would do it again, and it had also been the fastest way to tell a careful man that he had been found.

Both of those are true. There is no arrangement of them in which only one is.

*I did not have four days. I said so at the time and it was true.*

*And I have just found out what I bought with them, and it was a Saturday morning with twelve stems on my desk and her name at the gate for the first time this year.*

---

# Book Two · Chapter 48: The one who does not leave
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 1.0 · EN

## Day Two Hundred and Ninety · Monday 20 July

Jang had given him the other word on the Friday, and then Saturday had happened, and the flowers had taken up the whole of a weekend that the word should have had.

He had it written down twice, once in Jang's phrasing and once in his own, because he had learned in June what happens to a fact that gets tidied before it is used.

*Certain. Nine on the rota, counted eleven mornings across six weeks, never once ten. The Friday order has fed ten every Friday since the summer four years ago and nine every Friday before it. The grocer has not changed his round, his prices or his handwriting in six years.*

Jang had said one thing more on the telephone and had made a point of saying it slowly.

"I am giving you certain about a quantity," he had said. "I am not giving you certain about a person. I have never seen a tenth person, and I want that in whatever you write down, because in a year somebody is going to read your book and not mine."

* * *

Sang-hoon took a table again rather than a room, and this time Georgij understood it as the compliment it had turned into.

"You have had three weeks," said Sang-hoon.

"I have had three weeks and I have an answer, and it is not the one you asked me for." Georgij sat down without being asked, which he had not done in eight months. "You asked me whether the woman Jang has been watching is the same woman the trust was built for eleven years ago."

"I did."

"I do not know. I have not got one document that touches it and I am not going to guess at it in front of you." He put both hands on the cloth. "And it has stopped being the question."

Sang-hoon put his cup down.

"Say why."

"Because there are two of them in that house."

The restaurant went on being a restaurant, which is the only reason either of them chose it.

"Say how you know that."

"Nine people work that building on a rota. Four cars, two of them carrying two, counted on eleven separate mornings across six weeks by a man who never once went through the gate." Georgij did not hurry any of it. "The grocer delivers a standing order every Friday. It feeds ten. It has fed ten every Friday since the summer four years ago, and it fed nine every Friday before that, and it has never once gone back down."

"That is food and not a person."

"That is what Jang said, in those words, and he made me repeat it back to him before he would hang up." Georgij let that stand for a moment. "It is a quantity in the shape of one person, which arrived in the summer four years ago and has not left since. In the same summer a doctor drove a very long way to a house in Gangwon-do and put a lot number in the box where a patient's name goes."

Sang-hoon did not say anything at all for a while.

"You are telling me," he said eventually, "that the person you have been looking for since May is in that building."

"I am telling you that somebody has been in that building for four years who did not arrive in one of the cars and does not leave in one of them." Georgij put one hand flat on the cloth beside the cup. "I have wanted it to be her since Friday afternoon and I have written that down twice, in two places, so that it cannot quietly become evidence."

"That is a very careful sentence."

"It is the only careful thing I have left. I have been loud for five weeks and I would like one part of this to still be worth something in September."

Sang-hoon sat back.

Then he did the thing that Georgij had not planned for, and had not planned for because it had not occurred to him that the man was capable of it.

"Give it to me," said Sang-hoon.

"Say what you mean."

"I mean the house. I mean the woman at the window and whoever else is in there." He said it quietly and without any performance in it at all. "I have people. Real ones, not men who stand on roads. I have lawyers who can be at a district office at nine in the morning with a welfare application, a doctor and a court officer. I have enough money to make a provincial authority move in a week instead of a year. You have a notebook and a man with a folded map."

Georgij did not answer straight away.

"You would do that," he said.

"I would have it done by Thursday." Sang-hoon turned his glass a quarter turn and left it. "I have sat at that man's table for eight years and given away four things I cannot account for. I would like to take one out of the middle of him, and I would like it to be a person and not a document. I am fifty-nine and I have not wanted anything that specific in a long time."

It was the first entirely honest offer anybody had made Georgij all year, and he had to say no to it, and he took a moment before he did because it deserved one.

"No," he said.

"Then say it properly. Not the polite version."

"If your lawyers walk into a district office on Thursday morning, that building is empty by Friday night." Georgij put one finger on the cloth. "Not the woman. She cannot be moved quickly and a man who has kept her alive for eleven years is not going to kill her in a hurry. The other one goes. The one who did not arrive in a car, who exists on no paper anywhere in this country, who cannot be reported missing by anybody because there is nobody who has her name."

Sang-hoon did not move.

"You are saying I would rescue one and lose the other."

"I am saying you would rescue the one who has a name in a letterbox in Seodaemun and a trust behind her and eleven years of paper." Georgij heard his own voice go somewhere flatter than he had intended and let it. "And the one nobody can prove exists would be in a car within a day. There is not a court in this country that will order a search for a person who has no line, no owner and no date."

The words came out of him in that order because they are the order he has used about himself three times this year, and he heard it happen, and he did not correct it.

Sang-hoon heard it too.

"That is your own sentence," he said.

"It is."

"You have used it about yourself. In a shed, in May, and I did not say anything at the time."

"I know. I have used it three times and I noticed the second time and I went on using it." Georgij put his hands flat. "It is accurate. That is the only reason I have not stopped."

Neither of them said anything for a moment.

"Then what do I do," said Sang-hoon, "because I am not going to sit in a restaurant for the rest of the summer being told about a building."

"You do the thing you are the only person who can do, and it is not the house." Georgij took the folded sheet out of his inside pocket at last and put it on the table without opening it. "Baek Jun-ho has administered that trust for eleven years and eleven others besides. He is a solicitor with a practising certificate, which is more than that man has ever had, and it is the only thing in the entire structure that belongs to somebody who could lose it."

Sang-hoon looked at the folded sheet and did not touch it.

"You want me to frighten a lawyer."

"I want the opposite of that, and I am going to be exact about it, because if you get this wrong it is the same as the district office." He did not raise his voice. "I want to know what he has signed and what he thinks he has been signing. Not to threaten him with it. A man who has approved invoices for eleven years without asking has an explanation he has been living inside, and there is a version of Thursday where somebody sits down with him and finds out what it is."

"And if he goes to Choi Dae-ho the same afternoon."

"Then we find out that he already knew, and that is worth having and it costs us the trust." Georgij let it sit. "I am not going to pretend that is a small risk to hand you. It is the largest one I have asked anybody to carry this year and it is not mine to carry, and I would like you to sleep on it rather than answer me at this table."

Sang-hoon looked at him for a long moment.

"You have got worse at making things sound easy," he said.

"Yes."

"Good."

* * *

Annie was in the small room when he got back, at his desk rather than at hers, with the twelve stems still in water on the corner where they had been since Saturday.

She had the notebook open, which she does not do, and she had it open at the second page, which she has never done.

"You left it out," she said.

"I did." He stopped in the doorway. "Not on purpose. I would like that to be true rather than clever, so I am saying it before you ask."

"I know it was not on purpose. You have not done anything on purpose since Saturday morning." Annie turned the notebook round. "Sit down and tell me about Park Sang-hoon."

He told her, in order, and it took under four minutes, and she did not interrupt any of it.

"He offered," she said, when he had finished.

"He offered, and he meant it, and he would have had it done by Thursday."

"And you said no to the only offer of real help anybody has made you since October."

"Yes."

Annie put one hand flat on the open notebook.

"Say it out loud, the whole of it, because you have been carrying it since two o'clock and you have not put it down anywhere."

Georgij sat with that.

"There is a woman in a house in Gangwon-do who has been dying by nine days at a time since the spring," he said, "and a man offered me a court officer and a doctor on Thursday morning, and I said no, and the reason I said no is somebody I have never met and cannot prove is there."

"That is the whole of it."

"That is the whole of it. And I would do it again on Thursday, and again the Thursday after that, and I am not going to dress it up as a hard decision made well." He looked at the flowers rather than at her. "I chose the one nobody can see. That is the choice. There is not a second half to it that makes it better."

Annie did not tell him he was right, which he noticed, and which he was grateful for in a way he did not examine.

"You have not slept since Friday," she said instead.

"No."

"Then go up."

He got as far as the door.

"May I say one thing first?"

"You may say anything you like in this room. You have never once needed to ask that and I have never worked out whether you know it."

Georgij stood with his hand on the frame and found that the sentence he had been carrying up from the car was already halfway out of him.

"When he offered," he said, "I did not think about the house first."

Annie waited.

"I thought that if I gave it to him it would be finished. And if it were finished I would have nothing to bring up those stairs in the evening, and I had that thought before I had the one about the person in the building." He heard it land in the room and did not take it back, and then he stopped, which he does not usually manage. "That is as far as that goes tonight."

The room did the thing it does.

"Then that is as far as it goes tonight," said Annie.

She did not move from his desk and she did not close the notebook, and she did not say any of the four things she could have said, all of which he would have believed.

"Go up, Georgij."

He went up.

He did not write anything in either page that night, for the first time since the eleventh of June. In the morning he did not put a line through the gap or explain it. A book with an entry for every single day is a book somebody is writing for an audience.

---

# Book Two · Chapter 49: What he asked her
*Lot Fourteen* · Version 1.0 · EN

## Day Two Hundred and Ninety-Two · Wednesday 22 July

He went back to Mrs Bae on the Wednesday, in the afternoon, at the hour when the front of a restaurant belongs to whoever is standing in it and nobody else.

Annie had not told him to go and had not told him not to, and had said one sentence about it at breakfast which he had thought about the whole way there.

"You are going because it is correct," she had said, "and it is correct, and it is also the third time this month you have gone somewhere because the alternative was sitting in this house knowing about it."

Mrs Bae saw him through the glass before he reached the door, and did not look surprised, and did not look pleased.

"You have come back," she said.

"I have."

"Then you know."

"I know that somebody rang him," said Georgij, "and that it was you, and I have not come to say anything about that, and if you want me to leave before I say the rest, say so now and I will."

Mrs Bae put both hands on the desk with the book on it and left them there.

"Say the rest."

"You are not in any trouble and you are not going to be." He stood on the customer side and did not lean. "There is nothing you have done that anybody could put in front of anybody. You told a customer of nine years that a stranger had asked about him. There is not a court, a newspaper or a person in this world who would call that anything at all."

"That is not what I have been awake about."

"No. I know it is not." He kept his hands at his sides. "You have been awake because you gave me something first and him something second, and neither of them felt like the wrong thing at the time, and both of them cannot be right."

Mrs Bae looked at him for a moment and then, for the first time, sat down.

"You are a strange sort of man to have in my building twice," she said.

"Yes."

"Sit down, then. I am not going to talk about this looking up at somebody."

* * *

She made tea neither of them drank, which is what people do when they need four minutes.

"I telephoned him on the Thursday," she said. "Not the day you came. The day after."

"Say why the day after."

"Because on the Wednesday night I decided I was not going to, and I was very satisfied with myself about it, and then I woke up at four and I was not satisfied any more." She turned the cup a quarter turn and did not drink from it. "Nine years. He asked after my sister when she was ill. I do not know how he knew she was ill."

Georgij did not say anything to that, because there was nothing to say that would not have been a knife.

"Say what you said to him."

"That a man had come and described him, accurately, and asked me for the name on the booking, and that I had given it." Her chin was level. "All of it. I did not leave any of it out and I did not tell him I was sorry, because I was not going to be sorry to him about a thing I had done to him."

"That is more than most people manage."

"I have kept a book in pencil for twenty-two years. I do not need a lesson from you about telling people the truth in the correct order."

"No," said Georgij. "You do not."

She almost smiled at that and did not, and put the cup down.

"Now ask me the question you came for, because you have been in my chair for six minutes and you have not asked it, and I know what it is."

"What did he say."

* * *

Mrs Bae took a moment, and when it came it came in order, the way it does from somebody who has been over it.

"He was quiet for about three seconds. I counted them afterward, lying there, because there was nothing else to count." She put one hand flat. "Then he thanked me. Properly, the way he does. He said I had done exactly the right thing and that he would have been sorry if I had not rung."

"Go on."

"Then he asked me one question. Only one." She looked at the desk. "He asked me what the man had looked like."

Georgij sat very still.

"Say it exactly."

"He said: what did he look like. And I told him, and he said thank you." Mrs Bae lifted her hand and put it down again. "And then he asked whether my knee had been better this summer, because he asked about it in the winter, and I said it had, and he said he was glad, and he rang off."

The front of a restaurant with eleven tables did the ordinary things it does.

"That is the part," said Mrs Bae.

"I know."

"He was not pretending, and that is what I have been awake about, and I would like to say it out loud to somebody who understands what I mean." Her voice did not change at all. "He asked about my knee after I had told him a thing that must have frightened him, and he was not doing it to seem calm. He was glad about my knee. Both of those were the same man in the same minute."

"Yes."

"Then tell me what he is, since you know."

Georgij thought about it properly, because she had earned an answer and because he wanted to hear whether it held when it was said aloud.

"He is a man who is kind to everybody who is not the point," he said. "For nine years you have not been the point. Nothing he has done in this room was for anything. That is why you would put your hand in a fire for him, and it is also why he is worth what he is worth to the man he works for."

Mrs Bae took that without flinching.

"And somewhere there is a person who is the point."

"There is."

"Is she alive."

Georgij did not answer straight away, and the not answering went across the desk exactly as it was.

"I think so," he said. "I cannot prove it and I am not going to sit here and tell you a comfortable thing I cannot prove."

She nodded once, slowly.

* * *

He had two more things to say and he said them standing, because a man who is about to ask nothing should not be sitting down comfortably while he does it.

"If he rings again, tell him whatever he asks and do not be careful about it," said Georgij. "That is not a tactic and it costs me. It is what will keep this a thing that happened to him and not a thing that happened to you."

"And the second."

"You are the only person in this who has done anything twice." He said it flatly. "Everybody else in five weeks has told me one thing and gone back to their day. You told me, and then you told him, and both times you did it on your own and neither time did anybody make you. I would like it said in this room that I noticed."

Mrs Bae looked at him for a long moment.

"Do not come back here," she said. "Not because of any of this. Because you are going to want to, in about a month, when something has gone wrong and I am the only person in the city who will still talk to you. I do not want to be that for you."

"No," said Georgij. "You should not be."

"Then we understand each other."

At the door he stopped, which he does when there is one more thing, and she was already looking at him as though she had been waiting for it.

"He counted three seconds," he said. "You said you counted them afterward."

"I did."

"Then he took three seconds to decide what to ask you, and out of everything a man in his position could have asked, he asked what I looked like." Georgij had his hand on the frame. "He did not ask you what I wanted. He did not ask you whether I had given a name, or who I worked for, or whether I had been to any other kitchen, or whether I had asked about anybody besides him. He asked one question that only helps him."

Mrs Bae did not follow it, and said so with her face and not her mouth.

"He is not running anything," said Georgij. "A man running something asks four questions. A man on his own asks one."

* * *

He told Annie in the small room before dinner, in order, and it took under three minutes.

She listened to the whole of it and then went and stood at the corner of the desk where the twelve stems were, four days old now and beginning to be four days old.

"He asked about her knee," she said.

"He asked about her knee."

"Then you were right on Saturday and now you have got it from a second room." Annie did not touch the flowers. "He has not told anybody. He is standing in the middle of an eleven-year arrangement holding a thing that would cost him everything to hand over. He will go on holding it for exactly as long as he believes he can manage it by himself."

"That is a seam," said Georgij.

"That is a seam." She turned round. "And I would like you to say out loud, once, tonight, what you are going to do with it. You have known since Saturday and you have not said it, and I have watched you not say it for four days."

Georgij put both hands on the back of the chair.

"I do not know yet," he said. "That is the true answer and I would give you a better one if I had it."

"That is the true answer and it is not the whole of it."

"No." He looked at the flowers rather than at her. "The whole of it is that there is a man out there who has been kind to a woman about her knee for nine years. I am going to use that, and I have not worked out which end of it I am going to use."

Annie let that stand in the room for a moment.

"Write it down," she said.

"Which part."

"That sentence, in those words, tonight, before you have had time to make it sound better." She picked her pen up and did not use it. "You have started writing the tidy version again. Twice this month. I am not going to keep noticing it out loud for you."

He wrote it before dinner, on the first page, in the words he had used, and did not improve it.

*There is a man who has asked a woman about her knee for nine years and meant it, and I am going to use it, and I have not decided which end.*

And underneath, because he had promised himself in June that the page would hold both halves.

*Mrs Bae, and I have taken her off page two and put her on the third list, and she is the only person on it who did something twice.*

*She has told me not to come back and she is right, for a reason I would not have thought of. She can see the month I am about to have more clearly than I can.*
